INTRODUCTION

There are several possible sources for our knowledge concerning
prayer in the life of the intertestamental Jews. From their faith in
an accessible, yet transcendent, God--so evident in all writings of the
period--it might be deduced that they would be a people of prayer. Like-
wise, by a careful study of Jewish rituals we might observe their faith
in prayer and the style and structure of their supplications. But the
most valuable source of information is the general literature of the
period. In books of history, apocalyptic, wisdom, and religious-tale,
prayers occur in abundance. Because these are prophetic, spontaneous,
and unstudied prayers, they give a richer portrayal of the true life of
these people than do the formal, ritualistic supplications that have been
composed and phrased with care, It is to an examination of the former
types of supplication that we must turn for an accurate picture of Jewish
prayer-life.

Two things need be remembered, however, Many of the supplications
which appear in the literature itself are essentially ritualistic. On
the other hand, some of the lovely prayers which have been incorporated
in the Jewish prayer books were originally spontaneous utterances which
have, because of their rhetorical and religious worth, been preserved as
part of the liturgy. By a perusal of these latter, then, we can obtain
some insight into the nature of spontaneous prayers, while from many of
the supposedly informal, spontaneous ones, little can be gained because
they are actually ritualistic. Nevertheless, this paper, while not

ignoring the liturgical prayers, will concentrate upon the several

supplications interspersed within the less devotional general literature
of the period,

Although little will be said about either the Old or the New Testament,
it must not be forgotten that the purpose of this paper is to discover
the prayer-life in relation to the earlier Hebraism and the later Christ-
ianity. It was a period between these Testaments which saw many of the
concepts of ancient Israel come to fruition and which laid the background
for much of the New Testament, Neither of the canonical Testaments
displays prayers so prominently as does the literature under observation,
Yet petitions are implied throughout these two former collections of
writings, Both Testaments include prayers, but it is in the intertesta-
mental period that the greater number of them appear, We read that Jesus
retired to solitary spots to pray, but little is recorded concerning the
nature of these supplications. We know astonishingly little about their
contents and phraseology. But the experiences of the Jews had established
prayer as one of the tenets of their faith, They did not hesitate to
petition the Lord, and in home, synagogue, and private meditation, sup-
plications were frequent, The intertestamental literature, of all others,
wes the most filled with prayers. The Jews rewrote their ancient history
and portrayed the patriarchs and prophets as devout men of prayer.
Believing profoundly in the efficacy of supplications, they trusted in
the accessibility of God, and experienced Him personally in their prayer~
life. Therefore, one is warranted in turning to this literature for
expression of the Jewish belief in prayer and for examples of actual

supplications which they offered.

THH FREQUENCY AND IMPORTANCE OF PRAYERS IN EACH OF THE
BOOKS OF THE INTERTESTAMENTAL PERIOD

To generalize about the prayer-life of any people is difficult.
Nearly every race of mankind offers some form of supplication to the
Power or Powers which it believes to control its destiny. ‘The Jews,
as a peculiarly religious people, are surely no exception, They had
faith that God was an accessible Being, who has chosen Israel for His
own and would care for her throughout history, Any people with such
a faith might be expected not only to worship formally, but to pray.
Without studying the literature of the intertestamental period, there-
fore, one might predict that many prayers could be found there and
that they would exhibit a highly-developed religious life, But such
general statements can shed little light upon the distinctive and
unique qualities of prayer in this era. Only by observing the literary
products, examining both the nwaber and nature of the actual supplica-
tions as well as the references to them, can the Jewish life of prayer
in the period between the Testaments be fully comprehended. Further,
any development or retrogression in the concept and use of prayers during
the course of nearly three hundred years under examination, can most likely
be made apparent by discussing the various writings in chronological
order, beginning with Tobit (200-175 B.C.) and ending with IV Ezra (100 4.D.).

The entire plot in the Book of Tobit centers around two prayers,
one uttered by Tobit (3:1-6) and the other by Sareh (3:11-16). It is in
response to these two supplications that the angel Raphael arrives to

guide Tobias on his journey to visit Gabael. Both Tobit and Sarah, in

their perplexity and sorrow, pray for deliverance from their woes,

even by death if need be, The supplications are virtually equal in
length, but Sarah's is concrete and personal, while Tobit's is more ex-
pansive and general, even including some recognition that his sins

are related to the transgressions of the "fathers." The petition is

the same in both instances, however; i.e., if God will not deliver

them, then let Him take their lives, Tobit, a righteous Jew who has
become blind, prays to God sorrowfully when his wife brings home a kid
that he fears she has stolen. In the prayer he confesses his transgression
and those of his forefathers, asking that he may die, On the same day
Sarah is reproached by her father's maids for having married seven times,
only to have each husband die on the wedding night. Being thus falsely
accused of murder, she determines upon suicide but decides to pray instead,
She praises God, professes her innocence, and requests either death or
deliverance. Immediately the two prayers are answered, Around the divine
response to these petitions the plot is woven,

In all, Kaufmann Kohler lists three prayers in Tobit: i.e., 3:1ff;
8:5ff; 13:1-18,2 But others may well be added to this list. After he
has married Sarah, Tobias makes a smoke of the heart and liver of the
fish which he has previously caught in the River Tigris and drives away
the demon which has slain his bride's former husbands, Thereupon, the
bride and groom pray to God for mercy.” The next morning, when Raguel,
the father of Sarah, sees his new son-in-law still alive, he utters a fer.

vent supplication of praise and thanksgiving. Although no prayers are

actually uttered in Chapter 12, Raphael, while revealing himself
to Tobit and Tobiss, urges them to pray and give thanks, saying,
"It is a good thing to bless God and exalt His name, declaring God's
works and doing them honor, so do not be slow to give Him thanks,"2
The great prayer of thanksgiving which Tobit utters in joy over his cure
and the good fortune of his son constitutes the whole content of
Chapter 13. Because of the happy issue of the story, therefore, "the
old man let his soul go out to God in a hym of prayerful thankegiving."*
Although Walker here calls this a "hymn," we follow the lead of Kohler
in listing it with the other prayers, So as 3:2-6; 3:11b-15; 835.7;
8:16f.; and 1331-18 actual supplications are recorded, while in Chapter 13
men are urged to pray. Thus petitions in the book are numerous, beautiful,
and well-developed. Strict petition is, however, at a minimum, while prayers
of unselfish intercession, praise, and thanksgiving are predominant. The
whole of the book is "pervaded with a mysticism which finds its highest
expression in heart-felt prayer as the immediate means of communion with
God." ‘The author, believing in the efficacy of supplication to God,
gives numerous illustrations of prayers effectively answered.

The Wisdom of Sirach, or Ecclesiasticus (180 B.C.), although one
of the longer books of the Apocrypha, contains only three actual prayers.
The first comes at 2331-4, where, although the typical prayer~forms are
absent, Sirach directs his words to God and asks protection from his
own idle tongue, from ignorance, and from sensuality. Although Kohler
does not consider these verses to constitute a prayer, they shall be
entitled such, because they are preceded and followed by exhortations

in the third person directed to the reader, while they are them-
selves obviously pointed toward God, They begin with the words: "0
Lord, Father and Master of my life...." and they conclude: "And do
not give me up to a shameless mind,"1 ‘here can be no doubt, however,
that the two sections which Kohler does include (36:1-17 and 51:1-12ff,. )
are prayers, Although these passages do not strictly adhere to the
typical prayer-forms, the opening sentence suggests that the author
is now directing his thoughts not to the reader, but to God: "Have mercy
upon us, Lord God of all, and look upon us, and cast fear of you upon
all the heathen."* It is a prayer which seeks God's mercy upon Israel,
while asking God to raise His hand against the aliens. Rather in contrast
to what one might expect from the lofty Sirach, God is asked to destroy
the nation's enemies, In fact, He is requested to hear the prayer so
that the heathen may know who it is that responds to Jewish petitions,
Charles comments;
In the present subsection the writer pleads with God, in the
form of a prayer, that He will save His own, and strike fear into
the nations in order that all may know that He is God alone (vv. 1-5);
God is urged to assert Himself by gathering in the scattered nation
and by compassionating Sion (vv. 6-17). There are some striking
parallels between this prayer and parts of the synagogue liturgy,
especially the Kighteen Blessings (Sh8minéh ‘Esréh), some of the key-
worde of which seem to be echoed here,
The third prayer makes up the whole of the final chapter of the book,
and is one of thanksgiving and praise, Sirach recalls not only the various
times that God has delivered him from sin and destruction, but also the

response which has come to his previous petition for wisdom.

For these blessings, the writer gives thanks. Without inter-

mediaries or apologies, he prays directly to God in the first person
singular ("I", "mine", and "me" appearing frequently), thanking Him

for redemption from death, for "keeping back" his flesh from the

Pit, for standing on his side against the enemy, for saving him from
manifold sorrows, and for answering a prayer for salvation which he
previously has uttered, Charles places this prayer in the appendix of
Ben Sirach and seriously questions its integrity. If, then, of the three
possible prayers, one is an "appendix," and another is crude and vengeful,
it is a sad commentary on this otherwise great book.

Yet, we dare not ignore the references to, and instructions for,
prayer which occur in numerous passages throughout Ben Sirach, No
judgment of the book should be formed on the basis of the three above
supplications alone. Concerning practical instructions for effective
prayer, little is said in intertestamental writings other than Ben Sirach,
But here there are several suggestions, both implicit and explicit, which
are aimed at teaching men how to petition the Lord, Sirach says: (1) He
who provides for his father will be heard on the day that he prays;- (2)
One must not become discouraged over his supplications if they are not
answered;~ (3) The supplicant must not repeat himself when he prays;°
(4) One should pray for recovery from illness, but he must also give
the physician an opportunity to effect a cure of the disease.* In
addition to some of these more or less "practical® instru ctions, Ben
Sirach; (1) calls prayer a means of repenting and turning to God (17324);

(2) urges that when one forgives the wrongs done by @ neighbor, his own prayer

for forgiveness will be answered (28:2); (3) affirms that the Lord
hears the prayer of the wronged (35:13), These concepts, closely re-
sembling some of the injunctions of the Lord's Prayer, are exalted.
Sirach attaches a vital connection between ethics and prayer. The
supplication of the righteous man is heard, and if one forgives a neigh.
bor, his own sins may be forgiven through prayer. Not only practical ad-
vice, but the ethical demands of effective praying, are presented, Further
than that, however, is Sirach's presentation of a unique definition of
prayer, Though lacking the form of an official definition, the passage,
by equating prayer with the work of a craftsman, says much about prayer's
nature, Speaking of the common man who does the world's labor, Sirach
says, "Yet they support the fabric of the world, and their prayer is in
the practice of their trade."1 He asserts that the man without training
or spiritual development may still pray. He can supplicate with the work
of his hands; he can offer up his productions to God. It is not incon
ceivable that the author, rather than presenting what appears to be a
modern concept of prayer, is instead merely asserting that the craftsman
prays about his work, requesting that it be fruitful and satisfactory.
But because the author subsequently (39:6) refers to the spoken prayers
of the educated and wise, it is likely that he here means the unspoken
prayers of the craftsman, Undoubtedly Sirach thus employs a form of
poetic license, for prayer cannot be completely defined as "the work of
a man's hands." Yet the twentieth century has sometimes taught that work

is as efficacious as prayer, or that supplications need not be uttered,

the work of man's hands being all the prayer that is necessary.

It is therefore not to the actual prayers themselves but to the

more casual exhortations and suggestions concerning prayer that one must
turn to understand the full beauty and power of Ben Sirach's teachings
regarding these human outpourings toward God. Three prayers there are,
but numerous short injunctions, suggestions, and references round out
the picture left incomplete by studying the prayers alone.

As might be expected from the nature of its contents, Hnoch 1-36
(166-161 B, C,) leaves little room for normal orayer relationships between
men and God, Most of this fragment of Enoch both refers to, and has its
setting in, the supernatural realm, dealing not with history as it is but
with apocalyptic scenes and events. The book nowhere contains a real prayer
directed by a man to God, although there is one reference to such a
supplication (8:34), No actual words are quoted, but it is suggested
that, when the Watchers came to earth bringing sin, men cried to God for
protection. "And as men perished, they cried, and their voices went up
to heaven." Here is the sole indication that the author believes in
human prayers uttered to God. References to intercession, however,
there ere in abundance, but only one such prayer is given: i.e., that
spoken by the archangels in behalf of the men who are calling for deliver.
ance fron the sins of the Watchers,” It is a transcendent prayer,
filled with praise for God and with requests that He inform them--the
angels--what action to take. The remainder of the plot stems from this
intercession, for God gives detailed instructions to each of the arch-

angels regarding the suppression of sin and misery on the earth. One

er

of the themes of the book is that Mnoch is asked by the fallen

angels, led by Azazel, to intercede for them, His mission in their
behalf fails miserably, however, for the sins of the angels are such
that no prayer which they may utter themselves or which may be offered
in their behalf by someone else, can be effective. Instead, Enoch

is admonished in a vision to reprimand the Watchers, The author of this
apocalypse certainly sees prayer as no magical device, But it is not
warranted to assert that he has no faith in prayer whatsoever; it is
safer to affirm that his purpose actually offers little occasion for in-
cluding the petitions uttered by earthly men, for the setting of the book
is in heaven, and the theme concerns the final days of history. Let us
conclude, then, not that the absence of prayers proves the author to have
been sceptical of their efficacy, but that the purpose of the writer was
such as to make reference to them unnecessary,

In that section of Enoch which has been termed by Charles "The Dream
Visions," i,e., Enoch 83-90, one prayer is quoted, while two other
references to supplication appear. Forming in a short compass a phil-
osophy of religion from the Jewish standpoint, this section is divided
into two visions, "the former of which deals with the first world-judgment
of the Deluge, and the latter with the entire history of the world till
the final judgment."+ fhe second--and by far the longer--vision (Enoch 85-90),
with its peculiar symbolisms and metaphor's of the world’s history,
contains no lengthy quoted supplications and petitions, while the actual word

"prayer" does not occur, But this is not to say that the author has

no faith in petitions offered to God, In numerous cases Israel is so
oppressed by the enemy that one expects to find prayers for deliverance.
In at least two of those instances (87:1 and 89:19) a desperate, powerful
supplication is uttered, Nowhere are they called “prayers” and nowhere
are the actual words which are spoken quoted; and yet, Enoch says

that after the giants and bulls had begun to gore one another, "the
earth began to cry aloud."1 Furthermore, he continues: “Thereupon I
saw the wolves and how they oppressed the sheep exceedingly with all
their power; and the sheep cried aloud."= The Egyptians (wolves) have
subjected the Israelites (sheep), and the latter have called out to God,
who responds. Perhaps the use of animals as symbols for nations pre-
cludes the employment of "prayer" as a title for these outcries, but
they most certainly are supplications. The first vision (Enoch 83 f.)
contains one somewhat elaborate petition, in the form of a prayer by
Enoch that God may not destroy all of the men upon earth, but that

only the unrighteous will be subject to the divine wreth and destruction,
This is in a certain sense a "transcendant” prayer, one which praises
God profusely, affirming His acts as creator and sole sovereign of the
universe, and in which Enoch, without engaging in the customary self-
humiliation, asks God directly to fulfil his wishes.° Because Enoch
suggests that he wrote out his prayer for posterity to read, we are

confronted with a singular phenomenon, After he sees the vision, Enoch

arises and “prayed and implored, and wrote down (his) prayer for the
generations of the world...,#1 Although such an assertion is most

rare in intertestamental literature, and although the bulk of the
prayers are represented as having been uttered by the hero of the

story but not written down by him, Enoch boldly affirms that he put

his words in writing so that subsequent generations of Jews might

be edified. Scholarship is agreed that the Book of Enoch is a pseude-
pigraphal writing, however, and therefore the patriarch's words cannot
be accepted at their face value. Enoch did not write this book which
bears his name; so how could he have put the prayer in writing and
have incorporated it in the composition? Obviously the true author

is attempting to place authenticity and prestige behind the prayer-~an
excellent indication that he thinks prayer an important phenomenon

and the petition of a patriarch to be of exceptional value as a guide
to future generations, The author seeks to give to the words the ring of
authenticity and of authority. Hence, in these Dream Visions, prayer is
afforded a unique place. Although Enoch's act of imploring God is not
central to the plot or purpose of the writer, the fact that there is an
effort to undergird the prayer by asserting authenticity for it, forces
us to the conclusion that only the apocalyptic nature of the writing
prevents it from containing more supplications.

The Book of Judith, which chronologically follows these sections
from Enoch (150 B.C.) is as devoted to prayer as is the Book of Tobit.
The first seven chapters discuss the historical events which occur before
the initial appearance of Judith, the heroine, and which form the setting

for the tale. The only important prayer of this opening section is the

group supplication offered by all of Israel when it first realizes that
Holofernes is approaching (438-15),

And every man and woman of Israel who lived in Jerusalem, with
their children, fell down before the temple and threw ashes on their
heads and spread out their sackcloth before the Lord, And they cover-
ed the altar with sackcloth and cried earnestly in concert to the
God of Israel praying that he would not permit their children to
be plundered and their wives to be pillaged and the cities they had
inherited to be destroyed, and the sanctuary to be profaned and
reviled, to the delight of the heathen,!

Strictly a petition, there is little that is unusual in this supplication;
the people are but seeking deliverance, Yet, since the populace prays in
unison, it is a group prayer, a somewhat unique type of supplication

for the intertestamental period. Twice more in this opening section of

the book the Isreelites cry out to God for protection from the enemy and
deliverance from death (7:19, 29). But with the advent of Judith to the
story, prayers become more numerous, Not only does she affirm that God
will rescue Israel in his own good time if the people will pray (8:17),

but she implores Him to wreak vengeance upon Holofernes (931-14). After
she arrives at the camp of the latter, she goes each evening out to pray,
and when she returns victoriously to Israel, she offers a supplication of
thanksgiving, In fact, throughout the book prayers come at the climactic
points. When the enemy approaches, Israel calis for help from God (439-15).
Achior, the leader of Ammon, telling Holofernes that Israel is a nation
with a God-centered history, shows how her cry to Him formed the climax of
her efforts to escape from Egypt.” When this same Amnonite is brought to
the Jews after having been driven from the Assyrian camp for his champion-
ing of the Israelites, he tells them of Holofernes!' plans and Israel prays
again.” When Assyria bas seized the fountains and threatens Israel's water-

supply, the latter calls to her Lord,! Judith utters her prayer for
vengeance after she has promised to save Israel, * Having arrived safely
in the Assyrian camp, Judith departs each evening to pray for guidance
(11:17), As she makes ready to slay the drunken general, she prays
silently ( “said in her heart" ) asking for help to strike him down
(13:4f.), and then almost immediately bursting out into a verbal petition
saying "Give me strength, Lord, God of Israel, today!" When she carries
Holofernes’ head back to Israel, the people break out with praises to God
(13:17). Finally, the major part of Ch, 16 is devoted to an elaborate
prayer of blessing and thanksgiving uttered by Judith in the presence of
all the people. It is at the points of climax that these supplications
invariably come, The plot revolves about them, indicating that the un
known author of this moving story has the highest regard for and trust in
the efficacy of prayer.

I Esdras, as a writing practically contemporaneous with the Book of
Judith (150 B.C.) presents a considerably different portrait. The work,
opening with the account of Josiah's observance of the Passover (1:1-58),
traces the history of Judah through the Exile (2:1-4:63), the rebuilding
of the Temple (5:1-7:15), the return of Ezra to Jerusalem (Ch. 8), and
his reading of the Lew (Ch. 9), In the first three chapters--those which
deal with Israel's Exilic history and which tell the tale of the three
young men who contest with one another to name what thing in the world
is the mightiest--there are no prayers, But when Zerubbabel is appointed
the winner with his answer that women and truth are the mightiest, he

offers a short supplication of praise and thanksgiving,* for the king has per-

mitted him to lead Israel back to Jerusalem and to rebuild the Temple.

Upon the return to the Holy City, the people engage in a series of rit-
ualistic observances, which in some ways resemble supplications., They
offer hymns of praise and thanksgiving, but these songs are more formal

and legalistic than are the spontaneous prayers common to the literature
which we have been observing, Actually, therefore, no prayer is uttered at
the time of this jubilation,’ Further, when the Temple is dedicated (hs,
6 & 7) numerous rites are observed, but no prayers offered. After the |
migration of Ezra to Palestine, however, the supplicetions become more
frequent, He not only prays for protection as he leaves Babylon (8:53),
but offers intercession for Israel's sin at having married foreigners
(8:75-90), The final chapter contains no supplications of any sort. In
evaluating the contributions which I Esdras makes to our study, several
facts need be considered: (1) I Esdras follows the accounts of Ezra and
Nehemiah so slavishly that all its references to prayer are either suggest—
ed by, or copied from, these canonical books. I Esdras thus contributes
little to our knowledge of intertestamental prayer. Rarely can one determine
whether its supplications arise from the intertestamental period directly
or are borrowed from the earlier writings. (2) I Esdras exhibits more
interest in ritual and sacrifice than in prayer. (3) Extensive lists of
names are given in this legalistic book to identify those who have migrated
from Babylon back to Israel; reminding us much of the style of the Old
Testament Priestly ( "P" ) author; and suggesting that the prayers

which appear in I Esdras more closely resemble those of the Old Testament

than of the intertestamental era. (4) The book is valuable chiefly because

of its omissions, One is surprised to observe that when Zerubbabel and,
later, Ezra arrive safely from their journey they offer no prayers, <A
more appropriate time for thanksgiving could not be imagined, Yet they
do not pray. Further, at the end of the book, just after the reading
of the Law, prayers certainly are in order. But instead a festival and
celebration are held, The populace is admonished at this time:
This day is sacred to the Lord ,.. so go and eat the fat and

drink the sweet, and send portions to those who have none, for

the day is sacred to the Lord, Do not mourn, for the Lord will

honor you,
Although I Esdras has its values, its exposition of prayer life is not
of such transcendent worth as is that of the Books of Tobit or Judith,

The Song of the Three Holy Children, one of the short and fragmentary

writings of our period, portrays a more liturgical type of supplication,
Actually the Song is a Greek insertion in the text of the Book of Daniel,
coming after Daniel 3:23. "The title given is inexact: under it
are included two distinct pieces, namely, (1) the Prayer of Azarias, and
(2) the Song or Hymn of the Three Holy Children."© ach of the sections
is preceded by a short prose introduction which describes the setting of
the hymn, the Prayer of Azarias supposedly being uttered as the three
Walk about in the midst of the fire, and the Hymn being sung by the three
in unison as they are ultimately delivered from death, The date of the
evar is suggested by the words of verse 14 (Theodotion translation)
where it is asserted "that at that time there was no prophet, leader, or

sacrifice--perhaps between 168-165 5. c."5 Likewise, the references to

priests and Temple servants in verses 61f. (Theodotion) of the Hymn point
to a period after the purification of the Temple, i.e., about 164 B, C.
Therefore the Prayer of Azarias was apparently composed a few months before
the Song of the Three Children, Even though these prayers are largely
liturgical in form, they have considerable significance for our study,
not because they cast new light upon the nature of hymns of praise, but
because they are completely out of place in their present context. The
supplication offered by Azaries on behalf of himself and his companions
is more appropriately a national petition which (1) acknowledges past
sins, (2) professes present obedience, and (3) pleads for mercy. On the
other hand, the Hymn "is a doxology calling on all God's creatures to
praise Him; and its expressions are taken from the canonical Psalter
(see especially Ps. exlviii),%+ The editors who composed these two ad
ditions (two dates imply two or more authors) must therefore have desired
not only to expand the story but to attribute the miraculous escape of
the three Hebrews to the power of prayer. They must have felt profound
faith in the efficacy of petition to God by the righteous, and, observing
that the original Daniel does not sufficiently emphasize that fact, must
have inserted these elaborate prayers to substantiate their belief, It
was prayer that saved the three heroes; such is the message of these two
writings. Yet because the editors insert a prayer and a hymn that are
not at all appropriate, one may conclude that, rather than com
posing something original, they incorporated traditional national songs
end supplications. There is little that is unique in the style and form

of the Prayer of Azarias. The invocation recognizes the glory and
righteousness of God, Then the prayer continues with the customary
confession of sins, asks God to remember the covenant with Abraham,
describes Israel's present fearful plight, and concludes with a request
for mercy and protection, Although not appropriate to the situation,
and longer than the majority of those which we have thus far examined,
the prayer is rather commonplace, But not so the Song of the Three
Children} This Hymn possesses certain striking liturgical elements.
It is characterized by numerous repetitions of the same phrases, verse
after verse, and contains few of the accepted prayer forms, Instead,
it is exclusively a doxology, concerned with praising, glorifying, and
blessing the Lora. = There is no invocation, confession of sin, or
petition. Insteed, all creation is called upon to bless God. The
significance, then, of the two writings which constitute the traditional
“Song of the Three Children” is; (1) they introduce us to a more liturgical
style of prayer-life, (2) they present evidence that the writers
believed so firmly in the power of prayer that when they considered the
original Dniel to be faulty in not mentioning the petitions of the heroes,
they willingly appended some supplications, and (3) they suggest that
prayers and hymns at this relatively early date might be long and elaborate.
The prayers lack much of the spontaneity observable in the

beautiful petitions of Tobit or Judith, and they tend to be verbous, Note
the redundancy of the following verse:

We have sinned grievously in everything, and have disobeyed

your commands;

We have not observed them or done

As you commanded us to do, for our own good,”

Yet when compared with I Esdras, the prayer and hymn are enlightening,
It has been suggested that I Esdras copies the canonical Ezra and
Nehemiah so closely that it adds no prayers to the original text. In
fact, there are numerous places where we expect to discover prayers, but
do not find them, How different are the Prayer of Azarias and the Hym
of the Three Children} These two are definite attempts to add prayers
to a text which had originally omitted any reference to then,

The Prayer of Manasseh (150-50 B.C.) is another short writing which
is perhaps a Midrashic account of a petition offered by this wicked king
in II Chonicles 33:12 f. It is a beautiful supplication of penitence,
Pharisaic in color, and later associated with the prayer which King
Manasseh offered when in distress, Poetical in its form, it is the
only one of the writings under observation that is exclusively a prayer,
containing no prose narrative material whatsoever, There is a ring of
earnestness about the petition which forces us to the conclusion that its
author was sincerely penitent. It is a sterling example of the loftiest
ioe of soul -out-pouring toward God, fJFurthermore, the typical prayer forms
are carefully observed throughout. In fact, many of the standards by
which the beauty and balance of prayers have been traditionally judged
arise from this masterpiece. Ryle has actually summarized the contents,
outlining them as follows:

(1) The invocation: (a) 0 God of Israel (ver. 1), Lord and
Creator of the Universe (vv. 2,3), infinite in power (ver, 4) and
in anger against the sinner (ver. 5); (b) infinite also in mercy
(ver. 6), Thou hast proclaimed forgiveness for repentance, and
appointed me the sinner unto repentance (vv. 7,8).

(2) The Confession: my sins are innumerable: I am unworthy to
look upwards; I am justly punished, loaded with chains, in misery

(vv. 9-11).
(3) The Entreaty: I beseech Thee (ver, 11); I acknowledge
all (ver. 12); grant pardon; consume me not; nor let Thine anger
burn for ever (ver. 13),
(4) The Ascription: Thou, the God of them that repent, wilt
graciously save me (ver, 14); and I will praise Thee for ever,
(5) The Doxology3 Angels hymn Thy praises; Thine is the glory
for ever (ver. 15).
Here are all of the elements of a prayer in well-balanced form, and
eppearing perhaps as early as 150 B.C. Nearly all of the prayer-forms
occuring in other intertestamental supplications are incorporated in
this one to a greater or less extent, It is not a prayer for vengeance,
military victory, or Wisdom; it is a penitent petition for forgiveness
of sins,

The Book of Jubilees (125-100 B.C.), the first commentary upon the
canonical Genesis and Exodus, is the re-editing from the Pharisaic stand-
point of the history of events from the creation to the re-publication
of the Law on Sinai. The Book of Jubilees contains several prayers,
four of which are three or more verses in length: Moses’ short inter
cession for Israel (1:19ff.); Noah's petition for the deliverance of his
sons from evil spirits (10:3-6); Abraham's supplication for guidance at
the time of his departure from Haran (12:19f.); and Abraham's prayer of
his dotage, when, as an old man, he thanks God for long life (22:7ff).
Further, in a short prayer, Rebecca raises her face to heaven and blesses

God for giving her a pure son, Jacob (25:12f). Although no other actual

prayers are recorded, several enlightening references to such supplications

lgyie, "The Prayer of Manasses," Apocrypha and Pseudepigrapha of the

Old Testament, ed. Charles, op. cit., Ie, 618.

occur, Young Abram, having not yet migrated from Ur, observes the sin-~
fulness of the people: "And he began to pray to the Creator of all
things that He might save him from the errors of the children of men,
and that his portion should not fall into error after uncleanness and

vileness,"2

Although in many essentials the Hebrew idea of sin differs
from the Christian, the author of Jubilees understands that a man may
implore God's protection against wickedness, After the Lord has brought
Abram up out of Ur, leading him to the mountain, the young man praises
Him for His goodness,“ Besides, having praised God in 22:27, Abraham
blesses his grandson Jacob in words which surely more closely resemble

& prayer than a blessing; it is as though Abraham, through supplication,
were invoking God's protection upon Jacob, Having begun the blessing
with phraseology which might well mark the beginning of a prayer,

Abraham closes with a passage which is decidedly supplicatory: "And
bless him with all Thy blessings from henceforth unto all the days of
eternity, and renew Thy covenant and Thy grace with him and his seed
according to all Thy good pleasure unto all generations of the earth,"
Years later, on the day that Jacob escapes from Laban's servitude, he
blesses "the God of Isaac his father, and the God of Abrahem his father's
father, "* Then, at Bethel, remembering the prayer with which he was once
blessed, Jacob praises the God of his fathers,” Likewise, after his

transgression with Tamar, Judah seeks divine forgiveness. © Finally,

Jubilees 45:4 hints that the Israelites offered "grace" at the table in
conjunction with their meals, When Jacob becomes reunited with his sons

in Egypt, he rejoices at beholding them all eating together, "and he blessed
the Creator of all things who had preserved him, and had preserved for him
his twelve sons," Perhaps only under exceptional circumstances would the
Jews have offered such a prayer, ordinarily neglecting to do so. Further,

the prayer apparently has little connection with the food that is being eaten.
Hence, only loosely can the thanksgiving be called "grace at the table."

But this supplication, along with the others which we have observed, suggests
that prayer was a vital reality to the author,

If the Book of Jubilees, which is essentially a commentary upon
Genesis and parts of Exodus, can be shown to have altered the original
accounts, that fact will be a.significant indication of the religious
changes which come to a people through the centuries, In order to observe
such developments in prayer-life, one should compare the number of suppli-
cations in Genesis and Exodus with those in the Book of Jubilees, Immediate.
ly such a comparison reveals that several additions occur in the Midrash
which are absent from the original. Most notable is the intercession of
Noah for his sons, Although the supplication is contained in a Noah-fragment
and is perhaps not part of the original Book of Jubilees, the canonical
Genesis gives no evidence that the patriarch ever offered such a prayer.
There is, likewise, no indication in the 01d Testament that Abraham prayed
for guidance when he began his migration from Ur of the Chaldees, The Book
of Jubilees, nevertheless, records such a petition. Finally, Genesis is
wholly silent concerning Abraham's prayer of thanksgiving, offered, according

to the Book of Jybilees, just before his death, But this latter book also

omits at least one supplicatiom which occurs in Genesis, i.e., that of
Isaac for sonss> These observations suggest that: (1) Although the Book

of Jubilees contains a number of prayers, all of them are relatively short;
(2) The author, believing firmly in the efficacy of petitions, inserts them
in his account of the original Genesis, portraying the patriarchs as men
of prayer; (3) Prayers of praise and intercession are more numerous in the
Book of Jubilees than are those of strict petition,

The Testaments of the Twelve Patriarchs (109-107 B, C.), closely
related both in its date of composition and in its essential character to
the Book of Jubilees, is pre-eminent in the intertestamental period for its
teachings about prayer. In keeping with its exalted ethical standards, the
Testaments contains a peculiarly lofty doctrine of the function and value of
prayer. In eight of the twelve sections of the book, references to some
form of intercession, petition, or thanksgiving are given. The Testaments
of Gad and Joseph, however, present the noblest concepts. Nevertheless, there
is but one prayer in the entire book, that is, only one where the words are
actually quoted, ~ Other short allusions to supplications are made, along
with concrete suggestions about methods of petitioning God, but these refer-
ences do not record the words of the prayers. further, this petition, con-
sisting of but a single verse, is extremely concise, Having affirmed the
exemption of the godly from control by the power of the wicked, Joseph
"The God of my fathers and the angel of Abraham, be

utters a simple plea:

with me,#° If this single entreaty were the sole source from which to obtain

information, our knowledge of prayer in the Testaments would be meager, Sut

fortunately, there are other passages which allude to prayers, and although

the supplications themselves are not included, much may be gleaned from them,

In the Testament of Reuben, Jacob twice offers intercessory prayer
for Reuben; once when the latter is sorely afflicted with a plague (1:7),
and again when he has sinned with Bilhah (4:4), In the former instance,
Reuben was so desperately ill that if his father had not prayed for
him, he would have died, while in the latter case Jacob prayed that God's
anger over Reuben's sin with Bilhah might be removed. Here are examples of
the supplications of a righteous man in behalf of another who is less-righteous
than himself, In the Testament of Simeon there are no allusions to inter-
cession; but two unique suggestions about prayer are made, Simeon, when he
has gathered his family all about him, tells them that he has received his
name from the fact that his mother prayed that he might be born. He says:
“And my mother Leah called me Simeon because the Lord had heard her prayer"?
Although this is a play on the word "Simeon," it indicates not only that the
mother had prayed for the child, but that she recognized his birth as the
answer to the petition, Indeed, she must have possessed great faith in the
power of prayer. Moreover, because Simeon had hated Joseph so terribly
that he wished to kill him, God had withered his hand. Immediately he
believed that the tragedy had come upon him because of his feeling toward
Joseph; hence he besought the Lord to restore the stricken member, ©
Although the Testament does not assert directly that the prayer was answered,
it is disclosed that the hand was withered for seven days, which allows us
to conclude that at the termination of that period, it was healed, Here it
is not the righteous patriarch interceding for a sinner, but the sinful Simeon
praying in his own behalf, The issue of his prayer is almost identical to

that of Jacob's prayer which restores Reuben, but the method of achieving the
result is quite different. Simeon's is a petition, in the narrow sense of
the word, while Jacob's is an intercession,
Likewise, the family of Levi, gathered about the patriarch, is

told that, when he had seen all about him the sins of men, he prayed for
Salvation and deliverance from similar wickedness, | He was grieved over
the transgressions of mankind and believed that he might himself be saved
through prayer, Because this passage asserts only that Levi prayed in
order to be saved, and does not state from "what" he wished to be delivered,
it may be only inferred that he sought salvation from wickedness. But a
subsequent section phrased in the following manner, further clarifies the
matter:

Therefore the Most High hath heard thy prayer

To separate thee from iniquity, and that thou shouldest

become to Him a son,

And a servant, and a minister of His presence, ~
There can be no doubt that Levi here prays for salvation from iniquity
and that God responds to the plea. Elsewhere the author of the Testament
of Levi suggests the primitive belief that one must know the proper name
of a divine being before one can call upon it in prayer, In typical
apocalyptic style an angel had escorted Levi about, and the patriarch asked
its name. "I pray Thee, 0 Lord,” he said, “tell me Thy name, that I may
call upon Thee in a day of tribulation." Although the difficulties in.
volved in praying to some unknown Power do not completely negate the
possibilities of one's petitioning a nameless god, the author of the
Testaments considers it essential to know the title of the God toward whom

any supplications are addressed, So the Testament of Levi makes two
contributions to our knowledge of Jewish prayer: (1) petitions for sal-
vation from wickedness may be uttered and answered, and (2) the name
of the God must be known before one can pray to it. ‘The peculiar phenom-
enon of one man repenting while another intercedes for him occurs in the
Testament of Judah, The only reference to prayer in the entire Testament
suggests that Jacob had prayed for Judah because the latter's love for
money had virtually ruined his life, Three causes for God's leniency toward
the erring son then follow: first, that Judah had repented; second, that
the father had prayed; third, that God realized Judah's ignorance, Thus
while the prayer of Jacob was deemed efficacious, Levi thought his own re=
pentance to be similarly effective. To accept such teachings is to uphold
the anomalous contention that one man's repentance and another's prayers
May produce results. Whereas the author of the Testaments undoubtedly
accepts the traditional view that the man who prays must himself repent,
he also suggests that in intercessory prayer the one for whom intercession
is being offered ought to feel contrition,

The initial chapter of the Testament of Naphtali suggests that
Joseph and his brother Naphtali were like one snother because Rachel had be-
sought God to make it so, and therefore it implies that Leah's prayer for the
yet unborn Simeon had its counterpart in her sister's entreaty concerning
Nephtali.” Moreover, when the latter grew to manhood, he came to have great
faith in supplications, even dreaming one night of Levi's intercession in
behalf of the twelve brothers. According to the dream, a great storm had
arisen, and a boat in which the twelve were Salling was sorely threatened,

Therefore, Levi, girded about with sackcloth, prayed to the Lord in

behalf of them all.) Obviously, concealed within this literary device, lies
the belief of the author, whose suggestion that the priestly Levi acted as
intercessor is somewhat unique for the intertestamental period, Although
even in the thinking of the Testaments, Jacob is the supreme intercessor,
Levi must have renked similarly, Finally, and without further Sieectip es
passage which suggests that a man must be in a proper mood before praying
and ought not to allow incongruous elements into his mind while so engaged,
May be quoted;

For there is a season for a man to embrace his wife,
And a season to abstain therefrom for his prayer,“

Not only should a man set aside such a "season," but he should reserve it
for prayer alone,

Although Jacob's intercessory prayer for the recovery of Gad from a
serious liver disease is in no way uncommon ,° one lovely and unique idea
is expressed in the Testament of Gad: i.e., that one should pray for the
man who prospers more than he himself, The patriarch says: "If a man
prospereth more than you, do not be vexed, but pray also for him, that he
may have perfect prosperity."@ Even within Christianity there is no more
elevated doctrine of prayer than this, Supplication becomes a protection
against covetousness, But when this passage is amplified by one in the
Testament of Joseph which instructs the supplicant to pray for his enemies ,°
it is apparent that the author of the Testaments sees a vital connection
between ethics and prayer.

The Testament of Joseph, however, presents the most valid and consistent

teaching concerning the petition as reinforcement against temptation. Having

Called his family about him, the patriarch spesks of the Egyptian woman's
efforts to involve him in sin with her, At first Joseph prays in his
Chamber, apparently asking for protection against her wiles, Later the
burden of his petition is made clear, He seeks deliverance from her,
Persistent as she is in her machinations, Joseph is equally vigilant in his
resistance, Five times he prays fervently to God for help against her bold
approaches (Cf, 3:3, 4:8, 6:7, 7:4, 8:1), while in the finsl instance

he kneels all day and all night in supplication, As a result of his vigil,
Joseph, although being falsely thrown into jail, retains his innocence,

No more excellent description of prayer's effectiveness against temptation
has been written. The author attributes the success of the patriarch's struggle
to two things which run hand in hand, the prayers and the fasting. In
nearly all of the references listed above, fasts accompanied Joseph's peti.
tions, In vigorous terms the patriarch expresses his faith in the efficacy
of these elements of the religious life; "Ye sec, therefore, my children,"
he urges, “how great things patience worketh, and prayer with fasting."
Joseph believes the combination of prayer and fasting to be an efficient
approach to God and an adequate protection against even the greatest of
temptations,

The Testament of Benjamin presents little that is unique, merely re-
ferring to prayers for childbirth,® intercession by Jacob,* and Benjamin's
startling assertion that, through Jacob's prayers, he had himself vividly
and distinctly beheld Joseph, who was dwelling in Egypt--seen him exactly
as he was.” In a sense, therefore, the Testament of Benjamin, the last of

the twelve sections purportedly written by the patriarchs, serves as a sul

mary to this discussion, Although there is but one actual prayer in the
book, the allusions to various types of supplication are numerous and

varied. The book's rich ethical content carries over into its prayer-life.
Although there are some exceptions, the prayers are, for the most part,
either (1) the intercessions of righteous men for sinners, (2) the requests
of those who seek righteouness for protection against the wiles of sin and
temptation. Furthermore, one is struck by the number of unique elements
which the Testaments contain: (1) it is unusual to discover a recommendation
for the employment of prayer as a protection against enmity and envy; (2)
The persistent suggestion that diseases may be healed and sins forgiven by
intercession is uncommon, Finally (3) the Testament of Joseph is outstanding
emong the writings of the period for its affirmations that prayer and fasting
may be employed effectively as protection against temptation and vice.

Enoch 91-104, an autonomous writing of which the Apocalypse of Weeks
forms the heart, is an effort to answer the all-consuming question of the
age, namely, why the righteous man must suffer and be frequently subject to
the wicked, This section, written by a Pharisee sometime before 95 B, C.,
presents a mature answer to that problem, Although the wicked men
are seemingly sinning with impunity, their evil deeds are recorded every day,
and for these they will receive endless retribution in Sheol, But a day
ig approaching when these wicked will be punished even on this earth and
the righteous will triumph. From the eighth to the tenth world-week the
Messianic kingdom in which the righteous are to be victorious will endure,
Then will ensue the final judgment, the destruction of the old heaven and
earth, the creation of a new heaven, the spiritual resurrection of the

righteous dead, and the beginning of everlasting existence for the holy

children of Goa, + In such a book it is not particularly startling to

find a total absence of prayers. Although there are scattered references

to the petitions of the righteous and the intercession of the angels, no
prayers of any kind are actually incorporated in the text. In 97:3 and 5 it
is implied that the supplications of the righteous will be efficacious

in bringing speedily the day of judgment upon the sinners; "What will ye

do, ye sinners, and whither will ye flee on that day of judgnent when ye
hear the voice of the prayer of the righteous?" Furthermore, the following
taunt appears; "In those days the prayer of the righteous will reach unto
the Lord, and the days of your judgment will overtake you, "* The potent
supplications of the just man, offered on the day of the great assize, will
put to shame the sinful, At 99:3, moreover, the righteous are instructed

to offer their prayers on this great day so that the sinful mey be adequately
judged: "And in those days make ready, ye righteous, to raise your prayers
as a memorial, and ye will place them as a testimony before the angels in
order that they may place the sin of the sinners for a memorial before the
Most High," This is, indeed, onesuggestion that the righteous have been
offering up prayers for revenge and that they are now to be answered, In-
asmuch as the euthor of Bnoch 91-104 asserts that the sinful will be confound.
ed as a result of these prayers, several conclusions may be pon (1) the
supplication of the righteous man has imuense power; (2) the righteous
petition for vengeance, whether or not it is heard the moment uttered, will
be effective on that final day, for then such prayers will “reach unto the
Lord;"2 (3) the supplication of the just men actually facilitates the
process of judgment by becoming a testimony before the angels "in order that they

may place the sin of the sinners for a memorial before the Most Hich,"+ Further,
both 99:3 and 104:1 suggest that the angels operate as intercessors for men.
When the righteous offer prayers, the angels hear and respond by presenting
the petition before the Lord, Therefore, although this section of Enoch
gives no example of these prayers, either of the righteous or the intercessors,
it leaves some room for supplication, There are no prayers of thanksgiving,
praise, or repentance here, All are references to petitions for vengeance.
Also, there is an absence of that type of prayer so common to other apocalypses,
i.e., prayers for insight or Wisdom. Frequently, because there is some vision
to be interpreted, a prayer for understanding is found. But there is no
true vision in Enoch 91-104 and hence no such petition. Otherwise, there are
approximately as many prayers in this book as we have come to expect in
other apocalyptic writings.

In the charming tale of Bel and the Dragon, another of the additions
to the canonical Book of Daniel, there are again no real prayers. Nowhere
in this fragment, written about 100 B, C,. does the word "prayer" actually
appear, although in verses 38 and 41 there are short ejaculations ad~
dressed to God which mightconceivably be termed supplications. When the
prophet Habakkuk miraculously brings food to Daniel in the prison , the
latter says: “You have remembered me, O God, and have not forsaken
those who love you, "# Later, when Cyrus approaches and sees Daniel still
alive in the den, he shouts loudly: “You are great, Lord God of Daniel,
and there is no other beside you!" In the one instance the words are
shouted; in the other they are merely an affirmation, Although through-

out this paper a broad definition of the word "prayer" has been employed,

it is for several reasons difficult to classify these outbursts as "“prayers:"
(1) both are affirmations of God's greatness and trustworthiness more then
they are requests or aspirations directed toward Him; (2) the word
"prayer" is not employed; (3) the purpose of the book is chiefly to
ridicule idolatry, and any reference to prayer would be only casual or

at the most subordinate to the essential theme, But whether or not these
remarks be called "prayers," there is a striking contrast between the
story of Bel and the Dragon and that other addition to Daniel, the Song
of the Three Yhildren, ‘The latter is essentially a prayer, any narrative
material being almost wholly introductory and explicative. But the former
is a prose attack upon idolatry, and any prayers which it might contain
mast be largely extraneous. To remove them is in no way to alter the
plot. Indeed, the story of Daniel's conquest of Bel and the Dragon mst
be classified as one of that extremely small number of intertestamental
compositions which has little or no concern for prayer.

Another writing which should be listed under the same category is
the Book of Celestial Physics, found in Enoch 72.82, While the fragment
can probably not be dated, it shall at this point be inserted into the
discussion because it too leaves no room for prayer. No reference to
supplication of any kind appears, while the word "prayer" is nowhere to
be observed. In the opening sections of the book, which describe exclus-
ively the workings of nature, it is not surprising to discover no sup-
plications,+ But in the later portion, beginning with Ch, 80, a more
ethical interest appears. Although prayers might be more logically ex-

pected in this section than in the preceding one, none appear, Not only is

this the sole fragment of Enoch to omit all prayers, but it shares with only
a few other writings this phenomenal omission,

Of all intertestamental literature, I Meccabees (90-70 B. C.) casts
some of the most interesting light upon Jewish prayer-life, It is, indeed,
astounding to discover imbedded in the accurate historical accounts of
this composition a large number of prayers. Although no literature could
be produced by Israel without bearing some stamp of her great religious
conscience, I Maccabees, because of its astonishing accuracy of detail
and deep concern for national and international affairs is not strictly
a religious history. The initial section of the book (Ch, 1) closely re-
sembles any modern history and is free from supernaturalism or undue con.
cern over religious matters, Indeed, it might well be called a "secular"
digest of Israel's history. It is thus illuminating to observe the number
of prayers which the book contains; i.e., 3:44: 4350-33; 5333; 7337f.;
72:41f,3; 11:72; 12311. In addition to these direct supplications or re-
ferences to them, there are numerous suggestions that the Maccabees "called
upon the Lord.” But underlying the whole of the book is a positive
philosophy of history which makes the appearance of prayers in the ac-
count a normal consequence, Judas, when speaking to his faint-hearted

soldiers and followers, says:

It is easy for many to be inclosed in the hands of a few, and there
is no difference in the sight of heaven between saving through many or
through few, for victory in war does not depend upon the size of the
force, but strength comes from heaven....He himself will crush then
before us, and you met not be afraid of then.

Hence, most of the victories were attributed to the hand of God working, not

miraculously, but normally through the lives of warriors, affording them

increased strength and fervor, Because the Maccabees possess such a
philosophy, prayers for victory are in no sense incongruous, Neverthe-

less, it is curious that frequently the Maccabees pray fervently when

sorely pressed by the enemy, while at other times under similar circumstances
they offer no supplications whatsoever, At least four times Judas asks

God for protection against the adversary: (1) when the crisis caused

by the approach of Ptolemy, Nicanor, and Gorgias becomes serious (3:44);

(2) when Lysias prepares to attack (4330-33); (3) when Judas and Jona

than meet the armies of Timotheus (5:33); and (4) when Nicanor threatens

to destroy the Temple unless Judas is delivered over to him (7:41 f.).

In the face of other equally serious trials, however, Judas refrains from
such supplication. When he assembles the people to discuss the reports
concerning the oppression of Jews by the people of Gilead and Galilee, he
faces a severe crisis, Yet he offers no prayer, Further, he does not ask
God for wictory when he meets the king's army and elephants at Beth-zechariah, ~
Numerous other instances in which Judas or others of the Maccabees fail to
pray suggest some conclusions, The assertion that Judas offered these
supplications is either historically accurate or based upon well-known
traditions, The author of I Maccabees obviously is following no preconceived
theory about Judas' prayer-life, otherwise there would be more regularity,
more "reason" in his supplications, Before some battles he prays; before
others he does not. Since the accuracy of I Maccabees is not to be challenged
in most other instances, it should not be in this, Hence, if Judas was not
himself a man of prayer, he left such an impression upon his followers that

they believed him to have been, None of these recorded prayers is more than

four verses in length, that at 4:30-33 being the most elaborate and beauti-
ful. Further, of the seven passages which refer to supplications, only
three actually preserve the wording of the prayer (4330-33; 7:37f.; 7:41f.),
while the others merely assert that a prayer was offered, neither the
Phraseology nor much illumination concerning the contents being given,
No study of the prayer-life of any people is complete without observing
their recorded history, but when prayers are interspersed throughout this
hatbey even more priceless information may be obtained, I Maccabees,
although containing chiefly prayers of petition and intercession (i.e.,
Judas praying for his army), sheds light upon the problems of: (1) the
efficacy of prayer, (2) the nature of the divine response to petitions
for victory, (3) the SeieiiGe between the effectiveness and the length
of prayers, and (4) the employment of supplications particularly during
times of distress, The author of I Maccabees has profound faith in the
outcome of prayers for victory, but believes them to be answered with a
minimum of supernaturalism. Because the prayers in the book are all short
and all reach a satisfactory issue, it is evident that the author believes
the length of the petitions to matter little. Lastly, although this is
not universally true, most of the petitions, whether offered by Judas,
Jonathan, or Simon, are uttered in times of deep despair and crisis,
Manifestly different from the historical books of the Maccabees, is
Enoch 37-71, commonly called the Similitudes (70-64 B, C.). In conformity
with the other sections of Mnoch, these chapters deal chiefly with the
apocalyptic approach to history, while prayer is only a subordinate inter.
est. What the Similitudes do have to say about supplications, however,

is uniquely interesting. They are concerned with (1) the supplications

of the righteous, and (2) intercessory prayer. It is immediately apparent
that the Similitudes believe the prayers of the righteous to be heard in-
variably, an idea which is re-enforced by the assertion that all of the
prayers of the wicked, even requests for fereteisbe: will be forgotten
by God, going unheard, The evil ones are punished, and their prayers are
ignored, The supplications of the righteous will ascend to the Lord of
Spirits on the day of the great assize, and they will be heard without fail,”
Because of such promises the righteous should take heart. But the wicked
shall repent in vain, The Kings and the mighty will petition the Lord of
Spirits for mercy, only to discover that He does not heed their plea.” The
single prayer in the Similitudes whose words are given in full is the en
treaty of these mighty ones for mercy. But in spite of their prayer, they
are driven from His presence. Of the righteous it is said, however;
And the hearts of the holy were filled with joy that the

number of righteousness had drawn nigh, and the prayer of the

righteous was heard, and the blood of the righteous required

before the Lord of Spirits,
Of equal import in the Similitudes are the intercessory prayers, both of
angels and of the righteous dead, When Enoch is carried away to the end
of the heavens by the whirlwind, he sees the dwelling-place of the righteous,
“and they petitioned, and interceded and prayed for the children of men,
| and righteousness flowed before them as water, and mercy like dew upon the
earth,"° Those who have lived noble lives upon earth are authorized, after
death, to offer intercessory prayers for living men. But the Archangels
are the supreme intercessors, “And the third voice (Gebriel) I heard

pray and intercede for those who dwell on the earth and supplicate in the
name of the Lord of Spirits,"1 Throughout the intertestamental period
angels were commonly thought to exercise mediatory functions, These in.
tercessions generally assumed one of two possible forms: (1) the angel
might appear personally before God to relay the messages of earthly sup-
Plicants and to plead their cause; (2) the angel might offer some heavenly
sacrifice to appease Him, Here in the Similitudes, however, is a third
concept of mediation, The Archangel prays to God, in a manner similar to
that of human supplicants, Although he is one of the four "Presences"

who stands at the side of the Lord of Spirits, Gabriel himself prays in-
stead of merely relaying some earthly request or offering a sacrifice.

His activity is decidedly not the carrying of some huwnan cause to the
throne of God and there openly, in the character of an advocate, urging
Him to hear it. The mediation, more distinctly here in the Similitudes than
elsewhere, takes the form of intercessory prayer.

Equally unique is the suggestion in the Similitudes that the celestial
bodies offer praise and thanks to ne Lord of Spirits, Having observed the
orderly progress of the stars across the heavens, Bnoch says: “And they
give thanks and praise and rest not; for to them their thanksgiving is
rest."° Toward the end of the Similitudes, the patriarch expresses a
similar thesis’ all the natural phenomena of earth, air, and sea "believe
and give thanks before the Lord of Spirits and glorify (Him) with all their
power, and their food is nothing save thanksgiving: they thank and glorify
and extol the name of the Lord of Spirits for ever and ever,"5 Regard-
less of the absence of the word "prayer" from the above passage, it is

not illogical to interpret the author as suggesting that these natural

phenomena pray. Obviously one mst attach a loose definition to the word
to list the praises of inanimate, non-speaking objects as “prayers.” Yet
if such objects are able to praise God, they can also pray to Him. Because
praise and thanksgiving imply some form of hymn or prayer, the writer of
the Similitudes, whether employing poetic license or apocalyptic imagery,
esserts that even nature prays to God, It is somewhat unexpected, therefore,
to observe the almost total absence of prayers in these Similitudes,
Sithough there are numerous references to supplications, only one such is
actually quoted: i.e., that of the wicked rulers for mercy, and it to

not effective in gaining the desired end, God refuses to hearit. Yet
because of its lucid portrayal of intercessory prayer, along with its
suggestions concerning the petitions of the righteous, Enoch 37-71 marks

a mile-stone in the prayer-life of the Jews.

A few words may suffice to describe the concepts of prayer in the Story
of Susanna (100-50 B, C.), the first of the three Greek additions to Daniel,
Only one supplication of any sort occurs in the fragment, but although it
is short in length, it is a gem of concision and power, Susanna has been
falsely accused by the corrupt justices and seems destined to be stoned,
After the two men ahev testified, she utters a loud cry, saying:

‘Eternal God, you who know what is hidden, who know all things

before they happen, you know that what they have testified to against
me is false, and here I am to die when_I have done none of the things

they have so wickedly charged me with,

Not only is this a fervent petition, but it produces an immediate response
from God, She is being led away to death when the Lord, having heard her
ery, despatches Daniel to confound the testimony of the vicious judges.

Therefore, although only one prayer appears, it constitutes the center of

the plot; the advent of the hero is directly dependent upnon the prayer,
and the happy issue of the story rests upon it, The supplication, being
uttered in desparation, is not the ordered product of careful thought but
the fierce outpouring of the heart, As a result, Susanna cries aloud,
appealing to God's infinite wisdom and asserting her innocence of the
Charges, The Lord hears her cry and wreaks vengeance and justice upon the
wicked, Here, just as in Tobit and to a lesser extent in Judith, the happy
outcome of the tale is dependent upon the supplication,

Of all the writings of this period, however, II Maccabees has the
largest number of allusions to supplication, but in few instances are the
actual words of the prayers given. Only in two passages (1:23-29 and
1532224), or perhaps in three (131-7), are the prayers more than mentioned,
Although 1:1-7 May not be a true prayer, the other two assume the customary
concepts and structures observable in the more complete entreaties, such as
those seen in the Prayer of Manasseh, At the ceremony which attends the
purification of the Temple by Nehemiah, according to II Maccabees, the
priests utter a prayer while the sacrifice is being celebrated. Containing
elements of praise, affirmation, and petition, the supplication calls for
God to recieve the sacrifice, gather together the scattered people of Israel,
and afflict the nation's enemies,+ The prayer in its present form is
relatively complete, suggesting a highly-developed prayer life, The other
extensive petition is uttered by Judas when he is threatened by the forces
of Nicanor.~ Recognizing the effectiveness of God's intervention in Israel's

behalf in the past, Judas petitions Him to act similarly once more, so that

the enemy may be struck down, Although the passage is not as long as that

of Il Maccabees 1:23.29, these words of the Maccabean prince constitute

an authentic prayer. The more doubtful section to which we referred above
appears at the opening of the book where the term "prayer" is employed in

a loose sense, becoming virtually synonymous with the English word Wwish,"+
Having listed a number of the blessings which he hopes may come to the Jewish
brethren in Heypt, the author says, "This is our prayer for you here. "”

The form rather than the content of the passage militates against its

being called a prayer in the strict sense of the word, That is not to

Say that the Palestinian Jews had not been praying for their kinsfolk

in Egypt, but it is to assert emphatically that the passage as given

in the present writing is not strictly a prayer. Hence only two real
supplications are recorded in II Maccabees, Nevertheless, nearly two-

dozen times the word "prayer" or some equivalent expression occurs in the text.
Numerous allusions are made to petitions uttered by Judas, the priests, or
the people, but often no hint is given as to the length, content, phraseology,
or structure of the supplication. But two other passages occupy controversial
territory, being neither complete prayers nor mere allusions to some petition
whose: words have been omitted. One is the loud outburst of praise by

Judas! men when Nicanor is defeated: "Blessed be he who has kept his own
place from being defiled."? The other is the entreaty of the priests for

the protection of the Sanctuary from destruction by Nicanor: “Lord of all,
who are self-sufficient, you consented to have a temple for your habitation

among us; now therefore, holy Lord of all consecration, keep undefiled

forever this house that has been so lately purified,"+ Though somewhat
resembling true prayers, these passages represent incomplete, rather form
less outpourings of the spirit, Whatever may be the classification of the
five passages which have thus far been investigated (131-7; 1:23-29; 14:35f,;
15322-24; 15334), the numerous other sections which refer to petitions

give little indication of their wording or structure,

Only against the background of the philosophy which underlies II
Maccabees can the true meaning of the book's prayers be seen, The purpose
of the author is to affirm that God is ultimately the victor, that Israel
mast place her trust not in military arms but in Him, Almost invariably
after his victories, Judas recognizes that it is God's hand that has acted,
The author expresses this conviction in emphatic terms: “the priests
stretched out their hands to heaven and called him who always fights
for our nation,"*

The successful emergence of Israel after every crisis was
attributed not to the arm of flesh, but to the help which had come
from God in response to prayer, After Judas organized his forces,
and whoever the enemy might be,--whether Gorgias, or Lysias, or
Nicanor,--he was confronted by people who had just prayed,

In accordance with this faith Judas prays for help before nearly all of
his major battles. In an effort to demonstrate his thesis, the author
includes numerous references to supernatural phenomena, Whereas in I
ete heen petitions are answered in no extraordinary way, God frequently
responds with divine miracles in II Maccabees, The tendency to include
the miraculous in the narrative of history, ieacetacas is apparent in
the prayers also. Numerous miraculous events occur, many in response to
supplication, As a result of Moses" dens says II Maccabees referring

to a traditional story, fire descended from heaven to burn the sacrifice
wpon the altar, Purthermore, during the writer's own day, the people,
having seen mysterious manifestations in the heavens, pray that they may
betoken good,” Upon the appearance of Timotheus and his army before the
Jewish fortresses, Judas and his men pray for victory; then when the
battle becomes fierce, five heavenly figures on horses lead the Jews,
protect Judas, and hurl thunderbolts at the enemy.” Although in these
Passages the miraculous occureences are not strictly attributed to the
fervent petitions, the author surely implies as much, Likewise, when Judas
learns that Lysias is approaching, he beseeches the Lord to send some angel
as a deliverer, In direct response to this prayer "s rider, clothed in
white, appeared at their head, brandishing gold weapons, "4 Thus in II
Maccabees, perhaps more than in any non-apocalyptic writing of the period
--unless it be III Maccabees--supernatural phenomena are described,

In addition to belief in the miraculous and in God's direct inter-
vention, II Maccabees implies: (1) that hyms of praise may accompany
prayers (1:30; 10:7); (2) that prayers for the dead are customary; (3)
that Jeremiah operates as a heavenly intercessor (15:13); and (4) that
group prayers are frequent (Cf, 10:26; 11:6; 12:16; 12:28; 13:10; 15:34),
While supplications of this last type are not uncommon outside II Maccabees,
it is here that they are found in greatest abundance. Rarely does Judas
invoke God alone; usually all of the people, or at least all of Judes! army,
pray. Judas in one case specifically orders the people to unite in suppli-
cating the Lord for a day and a night; in response, the populace petitions
Him for three days without exiting.” Common prayers engaged in by all the

people, miraculous phenomena, entreaties for victory--all are cheracteris-
tics of prayer in II Maccabees.

The Wisdom of Solomon (50 B, C. to 10 A, D.) furnishes a further
introduction to the religious life of the intertestamental era, Since
the book is composite, exhibiting a sharp break in style and thought after
1131, no consistent teachings concerning prayer may be expected; yet there
can be no more exalted petition than that which includes the whole of
Ch. 9, Pleading his own weakness and the greatness of his task, Solomon
prays for the gift of wisdom, that it may be his bride to assist him,
The supplication, opening with a glorious invocation and continuing with
a curt petition, adheres to most of the accepted forms of style and
structure, except that it offers no doxology as its conclusion, merely
ending with the assertion that man is saved through wisdom. Although
most prayers contain references and affirmations which have theological
implications, Solomon's request for wisdom has an unusual number of
statements which suggest the nature of the author's theology. In fact,
if all sections of the Wisdom of Solomon should be lost except this prayer,
it might still be possible to reconstruct the writer's essential beliefs,
for the supplication contains varied references to and revelations of the
theology which underlies the whole of the book, In the invocation Solomon
eiticue tha nature of God, calling Him God of the fathers who created all
things by His word or wisdom, and suggests that He has control over history.
In that section of the prayer which is more strictly "petition," he not only
requests wisdom, but suggests that she sits at the side of God, + Further.
more, aS Solomon affirms his conviction of his own personal election to

the kingship (9:7,8) and ascribes wisdom to God, he also outlines her

nature (9:9), Finally, the last half of the prayer considers, along with
petitions, the various functions of wisdom and her relations to the ig-
norance of men (9:10-18). The prayer, therefore, in addition to the normal
structural elements observable in the majority of supplications, presents
an outline of the author's theology. Manifestly it is impossible to pray
without making certain affirmations and implying certain theological
beliefs, but to fill a supplication with as many such statements as ap-
pear here is uncommon.

Besides this one extensive and lovely entreaty, at least four
iiiwhuraderenses to somé kind of supplication occur in the Wisdom of Solo-
mon, but none of these record the words or the contents, Solomon in-
sists that he was originally like other men but that understanding was
given to him in answer to prayer. + He does not state whether or not the
lengthy supplication of 931-18 is a reproduction of this original petition
for understanding, but we may assume that it is. But more unique even
than his prayer for wisdom is his polemic against petitions to idols, With
blistering scorn he assails those who pray to the dead gods which they
have built with their own hasdece In phraseology which resembles that of

Deutero-Isaiah he says;

But miserable were they, and in dead things were their hopes,
Who called them gods which are the works of men's hands,
Gold and silver, wrought with careful art, and ijkenesses of beasts,
Of a useless stone, the work of an ancient hand,
Then he continues with the words: “And when he maketh his prayer for his
goods and for his marriage and children, he is not ashamed to speak to
that which hath no lite."* These attacks upon the folly of directing

prayers to idols are most unique for the intertestamental period. In

lime Wisdom of Solomon 7:7. 2mne Wisdom of Solomon 13:17.
othe Wisdom of Solomon 13:10. Sime Wisdom of Solomon 13:17.

earlier days such polemics were frequently directed against idolatry, but
in the Judaism of the first century before Christ such an attack is hardly
to be expected, Yet so dauntless is the assault made by the author of the
Wisdom of Solomon that he openly ridicules all supplications directed to
Man-made gods, The two other allusions to prayer are not as important as
those previously mentioned, The one represents Israel as having prayed to be
separated from the Egyptians at the time of the Exodus (18:2), while the
other refers to Aaron's intercession on behalf of Israel during the plague
(18:21). Thus the Wisdom of Solomon sheds light upon the prayer-life

| of the age. Supplications might be employed for curing a disease, obtain-
ing wisdom, or calling to an idol, But any petitions of the third type
were condemned as fruitless and invalid. Supplications could be effective,
but they must be directed to the true God, the Creator of earth and the
provider of wisdom,

Whereas supplications are important in the Wisdom of Solomon, they
are even more central as one of the major themes in the Apocalypse of Baruch,
Jeremish's amenuensis, Baruch, is described as a man of prayer, being
specifically informed that his supplications are as a strong wall to the
city. In keeping with his position as Jerusalem's intercessor, Baruch
engages in three long prayers and a single short one (21:4-25; 38:1-4;
4812.24; 543-22). But to avoid confusion, a distinction between two types
of writing in the book must be made: (1) a number of dialogues, or con~
versations--nowhere entitled "prayers"--between God and Baruch; and (2)

the four passages that are undeniably supplications, The former are informal
