communications between the Seer and God, containing several introductory
phrases, such as "And I said...;"2 “And the Lord said unto me,.,;"*

“and I answered and said...."° Not only, therefore, are these passages
composed in a conversational style, but they lack all of the traditional
prayer forms, such as, an invocation, a confession of sin, and a doxology.
In contrast with these dialogues, the true supplications exhibit careful
adherence to prayer structures, The three longer passages are strikingly
similar in style, content, length, and outcome, They average twenty-one
verses in extent, being, therefore, considerably longer than most prayers
of the intertestamental literature, Baruch first prays at length for the
rapid advent of the Day of Judgment. * Then, having beheld a spectacular
vision, he beseeches the Lord to interpret the phenomenon,” In a prayer
which affirms the greatness of God and ascribes to Him the control of times,
seasons, and mankind, the Seer bewails the temporality and mortality of
Ng Finally, Baruch requests God to interpret a vision,’ This is indeed
chiefly a song of praise. In contradiction to the other prayers, which
are heard by God and answered directly by Him, this final petition is ful-
filled by an angel called Ramiel, the presider over true visions, Having
originally appeared to the Seer in 55:3, the angel asserts specifically,
in 56:1, that he has come in response to Baruch's petition. Ramiel says:
“because thou hast besought the Most High to reveal to thee the inter-
pretation of the vision which thou hast seen, I have been sent to thee, "8
Moreover, in each case the answer, which is either a direct revelation of

God or the issuing of instructions, comes immediately. The prayers them

selves are supremely beautiful noth in content and rhetoric, being expressed
in simplicity end earnestness of heart,

Throughout the apocalypse appears a distinctive structure. Most
of the longer supplications are preceded by some ritualistic act and follow-
ed by a divine revelstion, "For the scheme of the final editor is first
a fast, then generally a prayer, then a divine message or revelation,
then an announcement of this either to an individual...or to the people
esefOllowed occasionally by lamentation, "1 Therefore, not only may a
predictable structure be found, but, because the passgaes referring to
fasts, visions, or lamentations are extremely brief, most of the book
takes the form of direct communication between Baruch and God, either
through prayer or through dialogue,

In addition to the four quoted supplications, there are numerous
references to prayer, especially in the final section of the book, which
Charles has entitled "The Epistle of Baruch which He Wrote to the Nine and
a Half Tribes." But even previous to that Sennen section, the author
of the apocalypse urges that Hezekiah's prayer was heard by God because the
former was righteous.” Both Hezekiah's righteousness and wisdom militate
in favor of his receiving a divine response to his prayer, “And the Mighty
One heard him, for Hezekiah was wise, and He had respect unto his prayer,
napavie ie was righteous."* Later the author materially alters his position
by suggesting that the prayer of the sinful Manasseh was heard, Thus, the
supplication of the wicked might receive an answer along with the prayer
of the righteous. In the "Epistle to the Nine and a Half Tribes" are

several familiar themes: (1) After having mourned over Zion, Baruch prays

for mercy from the Most High, asking: "How long will these things endure
for us? and will these evils come upon us always?! (2) the Seer's suggest-
ion that one must pray diligently and sincerely is a familiar theme.”
(3) Then he insists that God hears the prayer of righteous men, implying
that the intercession of the patriarchs, because they were righteous, was
heard, This is a recurring theme throughout our period, Finally (4),
the author, expressing a thesis similar to one in the Similitudes of
Hnoch, suggests that on the Day of Judgment it will be too late to pray.®
' Putting it forcibly, the book affirms that there will be no "place for
prayer, nor sending of petitions, nor receiving of knowledge, nor giving of
love, nor place of repentance, nor supplication for offenses, bes inter.
cession of the fathers, nor prayer of the prophets, nor help of the right-
eous."* In conclusion, then, the Apocalypse of Baruch furnishes a
brilliant insight into the nature of prayer in the later intertestamental
period. The author has profound faith in the immediate efficacy of peti
tions. The prayers which are quoted are gems of rhetoric and beauty. Al-
though they lack the conciseness of the supplications in First and Second
Maccabees, or the developed style of the Prayer of Manasseh, they are
worthy productions of their Jewish author,

While bearing a title similar to that of the apocalypse, the
Book of Baruch is, in several essentials, vastly different. It is chiefly
liturgical, reflecting the age which saw the fall of Jerusalem in 70 A, D,
Of composite authorship, the book is a collection of different pieces
from various times, But it is the first of the four sections (i.e., 1:1-3:8)°
which is of most interest to the student of prayer, for this introductory

-47~

division contains praises to God, a confession of sin, and an extended prayer
for Divine mercy, While the style of this section is essentially liturgi-
cal, it exhibits many of the prayer forms found in the more spontaneous
prophetic prayers. Praise, confession, expression of humility, and re.
quests for Divine mercy are all constituents of this prayer. Three of its
characteristics are unique; (1) the extended length; (2) the absence
of a doxology; (3) the inclusion of scriptural passages within the suppli-
cation itself. It is uncommon to discover in a book of such relative
brevity a prayer that constitutes nearly a fifth of the entire length.
As has been seen, the opening section includes, besides the prayer, both
praise to God and confession of sin. Therefore, because these elements
of praise and confession occur both within and without the supplication
itself, the entire first division of the book is prayer-like in structure,
The absence of a doxology is somewhat unusual in a prayer as complete as this
one. Rather than concluding in some outburst of praise, this petition ends
with the following words: “here we are today in exile, where you have
scattered us, to be reproached and cursed and condemned for all the in-
iquities of our forefathers who rebelled against the Lord our God." Such
an abrupt termination might be an indication either that the prayer was
originally composed without a doxology, or that the concluding passages
have been lost. The fact that a poetical section of wisdom literature, dif.
ferent in style and content from the preceding verses, immediately follows
the prayer, makes more plausible the belief that the doxology, although origi-
nally a part of the supplication, has been omitted by the final editor.
Finally, the inclusion of two scriptural passages within the text of the

prayer itself is unique, God's previous commands mediated by Jeremiah to
Israel are recalled and quoted (2321-23), along with His warning to Moses
concerning the future sins of Israel (2:29.35), The Lord had ordered
Israel through the words of Jeremich to serve the King of Babylon, but
the people had disobeyed, Subsequently, the words of Jeremiah are quoted,
and the Jewish disobedience is pointed out. Later, the predictions given
to Moses by God are recorded, Although such scriptual references included
Within a supplication are perhaps evidence of its liturgical nature, such a
phenomenon is unusual for our period. Because this petition exhibits,
therefore, several unique characteristics along with its adherence to
traditional forms, it contributes to our general kmowledge of the nature
of intertestamental prayer.

Several of the principles which have been observed in the Book of
Baruch apply likewise to the writing which shall be considered as the
last of the compositions of our period: i.e., the Ezra Apocalypse, more
frequently called IV Ezra. Because this, too, is a composite work, few
general principles concerning its concepts about prayer may be discovered,
Considered technically, Ch. 1-2 constitute II Esdras, 3-14 equal IV isdras ,
and 15-16 make up V Esdras. The first two chapters contain no supplications,
although the word "prayer," or its equivalent, twice appears; once in
an obscure passage which suggests that God Himself has prayed for Israel
to become His people (1:28); again when the Seer is advised to pray for
“few days," i.e., for the rapid termination of time (2:13). The final
section (Ch, 15-16) is one of those rare passages in intertestamental

literature where neither a supplication nor the word "prayer" are found,

Although these closing chapters are lengthy discussions of the evils and
divine judgments which are imminent, they are concerned with neither ex-
amples of nor theories about prayer.

But the important sections of IV Ezra (Ch, 3-14) are surely prayer-
centered, If all of the utterances which the Seer directs heavenward,
either to God or to Uriel, be called "prayers," there are ixtenaty dozens
of them, But when one, by a careful selection, rules out all of those
passages which describe actual dialogues between Ezra and the divine beings,
there are at least nine prayers (334-36; 5323.30; 6338.59; 8320-36; 10338;
1237-9; 13:14-20; 14:19-26), With the exception of Bzra's glorious inter-
cession for sinners (8:20-36), there is such an absence of the usual prayer
structures, however, that some of the passages listed above have been
chosen almost arbitrarily, such selection being based upon either Oester.
ley's listing them as prayers or upon the suggestions with the text itself
which make the general nature of the passages clear, The ever-recurring
dialogues between the Seer and God or the angel confuse the matter, for
not only do they precede and follow virtually every supplication, but they
are frequently so elaborate and well-fashioned as to resemble prayers,

While not as evident as those in the Apocalypse of Baruch, rather
clear-cut divisions occur in IV Bzra, each either beginning with, or includ
ing, ats supplication. At the opening of the first, second, third, and
fourth visions, prayers occur, Although these are not of such uniform
length as those found in the Apocalypse of Baruch, they express interrelated
themes. Because Bzra's supplications are little more than blunt requests

for insight, frequently put in the form of direct questions, they omit most

all of the elements found in the typical prayers of the period. Praise,
penitence, confession, and doxology are strangely lacking, Usually the
petitions, after dwelling at length upon problems of history, creation, or
nature, conclude with the posing of some perplexing question. The pessimism
which pervades the writing is reflected in the prayers, whose chief burden
is to inquire why the righteous Israel must suffer oppression at the hands
of the wicked heathen, The first of these prayers presents a retrospective
view of the world's history from creation, continuing through the experiences
of the Jewish patriarchs and the Exodus, down to the Exile, and concluding
with the question: Is Israel mre sinful than Babylon that she should be
thus punished?! In response to the query the angel Uriel appears before
Ezra. The second supplication describes God as ruler of nature and as One
who has chosen certain animals, a particular city (Zion), and a single people
(Isreel).” the petition is short and beautifully phrased, but again,
it is essentially a request for understanding, The third prayer outlines
the seven days of creation, concluding with the inevitable problem: If
the world has been created for Israel's benefit, why must she be oppressed?”
The final petition in this series, recalling the divine mercies of the past,
especially the imparting of the Law, and lamenting the failure of Israel to
obey the statutes, insists that the Law is eternal and will endure, * Be~
cause sieve is neither request nor petition herein, this is a most peculiar
prayer. Yet it may be included as one of the four major supplications, all
of which are somewhat similar in theme.

Roughly corresponding to this symmetrical pattern are the three

requests by Uriel that the Seer spend a period of fasting and prayer in order

that further revelations may be vouchsafed to him, At the conclusion of
three of the visions the angel commands Ezra to fast and pray (5:13; 6:31;
and 9:23ff), Coming at the end of one vision, this command thus precedes
the prayer which begins the next vision, Therefore, in three instances the
prayers which have been observed above are preceded by this injunction
that the Seer fast and pray. After concluding his tale of the horrible
events which are to occur, Uriel says: "These things I am permitted to
tell you, but if you pray again and weep as you do now, and fast seven
days, you will again hear greater things than these," Only in 9:23 are the
| instructions varied; because the Seer is instructed to eat the flowers
of the field, the fast is not to be complete. Therefore, the visions
which are described come as a direct result of these periods of fasting
and praying.
But at Ch, 11 comes a break in the practices concerning prayer.
In the preceding section (Ch, 4-10) the Seer first poses his question and
only then receives the revelation, The reverse, however, is true of the
eagle vision, where the dream comes at the outset and is only then followed
by Ezra's prayer for interpretation. Moreover, prayer styles are consider.
ably different in this section. The supplications are less elaborate and
verbous, After Ezra in his dream has been confronted with the amazing
vision, he awakes and prays for strength and insight, seying:
© Lord that bearest rule, if I have found favor in thy sight,

and if I em justified with thee above many others, and if my

prayer indeed be come up before thy face; strengthen me then, and

shew me thy servant the interpretation and plain meaning of this

fearful vision, that thou mayest perfectly comfort my soul, For
thou hast judged me worthy to shew me the end of time and the

last times,

How different is this supplication from that which precedes the first of
the visions at 3:4-36! In the former the long historical passages are
absent, just as are the perplexing queries which form the conclusion of
5:34.36, Moreover, the prayer quoted above (12:7-9) adheres far more closely
to the strict prayer forms than do any that have thus far been examined,
Hints of praise and humiliation are more evident here than elsewhere,

while the actual petition is made more specific and concrete, But there

is not a complete break between the four prayers which precede the first
visions on the one hand and this one which follows the dream on the other

. hand. The invocation in three of them is indentical, i.e., "O Lord

that bearest rule," (Cf. 3:4; 5:23; and 12:7). Likewise, after re-
ceiving the vision of the man from the sea (13:1-13), Ezra utters a short
prayer similar to that of 12:7-9. It too exhibits more normal prayer
forms, except that it concludes with a short parenthetical passage out—
lining the relative misfortune of those who will live to see the conclusion
of the present world-order and those who will die before the end,

But, in addition to these differences in prayer style which appear
after Ch. 1l, the admonitions which are given to the Seer are different.
After hearing the interpretation of the Hagle Vision, he is instructed to
wait seven days for further revelation, but is not told to fast or pray.”
When the Vision of the Man from the Sea has been interpreted to him, the
Seer, in response to the suggestion of the Most Hizgn, goes and sits three
days awaiting additional illuwmination.* In neither of these instances
does the Seer pray as he has done thrice before, The being who interprets

the visions, moreover, is never specifically named in this section, but

from implication it is apparent that not Uriel, but the Most High Himself
enswers the prayers, Therefore, in structure and content the prayers,
along with the context in which they are imbedded, are dissimilar from
those in the portions of the book prior to Ch. ll.

One additional supplication of Ezra's must be considered, In all
of the intertestamental literature there is no more superb prayer than
that found in IV Ezra 8:19-36, Liturgical in style, stately in phraseology,
and powerful in emotional value, it serves as a fitting climax to any study
of the prayers in this great literature, There is first an address to
God, containing a detailed list of the divine attributes, and striking the
note of praise which is lacking in so many of the supplications in IV Ezra:

O Lord, thou that abidest forever, whose eyes are exalted, and

whose chambers are in the air; whose throne is inestimable;

whose glory may not be comprehended; before whom the hosts of

angels stand with trembling, at whose bidding they are changed

to wind and fire; whose word is sure, and sayings constant;

whose ordinance is strong, and commandment fearful; whose look

drieth up the depths, and whose indignation maketh the mountains

to melt away, and whose truth beareth witness; hear, 0 Lord the

prayer of thy servant..oet
After these exalted and elaborate affirmations of God's power and majesty,
the Seer humbles himself, calling himself "thy servant." Then follow the
petitions, interspersed with confession and intercession, The Seer pleads
with God to overlook the sins of Israel and to judge her on the basis of
the deeds of the righteous rather than upon those of the wicked, He
entreats: "Think not upon those that have walked feignedly before thee;
but remember them which have willingly known thy fear,"* Then he calls
attention to the universal sinfulness of mankind, recognizing that he and

his fathers have sinned, and pleading that God may be merciful. With pro-

foundly stirring words, he brings the prayer to a climax:
For what is man, that thou shouldest take displeasure at

him? or what is a corruptible race, that thou shouldest be so

bitter toward it? For in truth there is no man among then that

be born, but he hath dealt wickedly; and ae them that have

lived there is none which hath not done amiss,
There is deep pathos in these words, ‘The Seer recognizes his own sinfulness
and that of all men in the sight of God's glory. He returns to his
basic question: Why should Israel be punished for her sin any more than
other nations? for all the world is sinful; every race is wicked! Yet,
the final verse, which in many ways resembles a doxology, suggests that
if God is merciful, His praises shall be declared, "For in this, 0 Lord,.
thy righteousness and thy goodness shall be declared, if thou be merciful

unto them which have no store of good works, "©

The Seer therefore prays that God will shed His grace upon the sinner,
especially upon the one who has no merits of his own, The outstanding elements
in the prayer are its simplicity, sincerity, emotional quality, and religious
insight. God is appealed to as a Lord of mercy and righteousness. Such
a supplication, along with those in the Prayer of Manasseh and the Wisdom
of Solomon, mark the apex of Jewish devotional life, It is an outpouring
of the spirit which is found nowhere else in this apocalypse. Indeed, it
serves aS a splendid conclusion to our study of the prayers in the various
books. Not everywhere throughout this literature are prayers of such lofty
calibre to be found. Yet one is impressed that here and there, in the
earlier books as well as in the later ones, these glorious petitions,

confessions, and intercessions stand out above all their surroundings as

gems in a drab setting.

-55—

TH2 TYPES OF PRAYER FOUND IN THE
INTERTESTAMENTAL LITERATURE

As might logically be expected, petitions constitute, in this
literature, the most numerous class of prayers, There are requests for:
(1) short life and the rapid advent of the Day of the Lord; (2) increase
in the nation's population; (3) mercy; (4) protection of the Temple
from pollution; (5) vengeance and victory; (6) Wisdom and understanding,
Because the petitions for victory and for Wisdom are somewhat more numerous
_ and distinctive, independent discussions will be reserved for them, But
the remainder of the supplications were uttered under unique conditions and
are but examples of the multitudinous forms of petition, Any examination
of the types of prayer employed by the intertestamental Jews must begin,
therefore, with a discussion of these varied illustrations of petition.

The astonishing reference to a prayer for short life appears in
IV Bzra 2:13: “Ask and you will receive; pray for few days for your.
selves, that they may be shortened; the kingdom is already prepared for
yous; be on the watch." Not only does such a citation suggest that God
hears a request and surely grants it, but it proposes that men should pray
for: (1) the termination of life; (2) the speedy approach of the
Day of the Lord, This verse actually states only that men should pray
for short life and does not explicitly enjoin them to request the advent

of the Kingdom of the Lord, Yet from two clues it is seen that this

passage urges them to pray for such a "Coming." first, the imminent ap-

proach of the Kingdom is vividly described in that very passage. Because

the Kingdom is prepared for men, they must be on the watch for it. In

the verse, therfore, which urges men to pray for short life appears also
the prediction of the arrival of the Kingdom, Second, the word employed
is not "self" but "selves," implying that men in the plural, taken as a class,
are to pray. Why should all men pray for short life? Undoubtedly it is be-
cause they will receive the Kingdom when they die, To them, at least, the
Kingdom will come more quickly if the length of their lives is shortened;
hence, IV Ezra 2:13 is a petition for the rapid termination of life and the
consequent coming of a Kingdom which is already prepared.

Equally interesting and exceptional is the prayer of Moses, asking
God to increase the population of Israel, Only indirectly is such a sup.
Plication mentioned, where, in the Assumption of Moses, the following words
appear: “For of them there will be 600,000 men, for these have multiplied
to this degree through thy prayers, (my) lord Moses," Moses, seeking the
security of Israel, prays that her numbers may be increased. The historical
basis of that petition is obscure, so that the author of the Assumption may
conceivably tive (1) based the tale upon authentic Old Testament accounts;
(2) fabricated the story in its entirety. Yet, by the very act of including
such a tale, the author indicates his belief in both the efficacy and possi-
bility of such a prayer. Although the custom of praying for offspring was
not a novelty--Hannah, centuries before had prayed for a son--it is signif.
icant to discover reference to the practice in the later days of the inter-
To have faith in a prayer for increased population is to

mous author of the Assumption of Moses obviously admits this possibility,
Likewise the Apocelyose of Baruch alludes to a Perallel petition: "and ye
women, pray not that ye may bear."2 Such a supplication again suggests be-
lief that God has a direct control over the generative process. Whatever
the theological connotations, however, at least two intertestamental writers
believed that petitions directed to God could influence population.

In the same Apocalypse of Baruch a petition for mercy appears. A
prayer of considerable length, it asks God, after a considerable section of
praise and self-humiliation, for succour and protection.” In large
' measure it is divorced from any concrete situation, being, as it were,
independent of a practical context. It follows a period of fasting, and
merely evokes God's mercy for sinful, lowly man, Although it thus does
not arise from any concrete state of affairs, it is truly a prayer for
mercy. Yet, just like all prayers that have not one specific goal but
many goals centering in a single urge and undertone of feeling, this sup-
plication is a rambling petition for aid, protection, mercy, and salvation,
The supplicant senses his humility when contrasted with the splendor
of God, and from that feeling utters a number of fervent petitions,
each in a sense related to, but not identical with, the other. It is,
therefore, a petition directed not so much to the attainment of a particu-
lar goal as to the expression of an inward feeling and urge, Nevertheless,
this series of petitions, all related and all uttered from one outpouring

of the spirit, actually constitute e prayer for mercy.

First, Second, and Third Maccabees all contain petitions for
protection of the Temple against pollution, I Maccabees 7:33-50 re-
counts the story of Nicanor's threat to destroy the sanctuary unless
Judas is surrendered and it quotes the prayers uttered by the priests,
and later by Judas, for the protection of the sacred site, II Maccabees
14:33-15:336 presents a closely parallel account, in which the Jewish
heroes again ask God to protect the Temple from the heathen. II Maccabees
3313-30 tells a somewhat altered but related tale. Heliodorus attempts
to collect tribute money and to loot the Temple-treasury, only to be
deterred by the mysterious rider who strikes him down and threshes him,
it is in III Maccabees 1:10-2:24 that the parallel to that story appears,
Although in various non-essentials this account differs from that of
II Maccabees 3:13-30, the theme is virtually identical. This time it
is the heathen Philopater who attempts to effect an entry into the sanctu-
ary, but without success, The priests and the populace petition God for
preservation of the holy-of-holies and He responds. Hence, although
details of the episodes are dissimilar, one well~attested form of petition
was the request for preservation of the Temple's sanctity.

Two of the most common types of petition were requests for (1)
Wisdom, and (2) vengeance and victory. It is in the later literature
of the intertestamental period that the prayers for Wisdom and under-
standing appear; i.e., in the Wisdom of Solomon, the Apocalypse of Baruch,
and IV Ezra. Of the Wisdom of Solomon 9:1-18, G. H. Box says: "In the...
beautiful prayer Solomon prays for the gift of wisdom, pleading his own

human weakness and the greatness of his task," Likewise, in Wisdom 7:7

appear the words: "Therefore I prayed, and understanding was given me;

I called, and the spirit of wisdom came to me," Although no actual words
of any prayer are here quoted, the reference implies not only that he con-
siders "understanding" and "wisdom" to be relatively synonymous concepts,
but that both of them have come to him as a result of prayer, It is

for the hypostasized Wisdom, the one who may be sent to dwell with men,
that Solomon prays. From his youth up he has sought to take her for

his pride, t Now he asks God to give her to him, © The petition is
answered; Wisdom is a gift that God can and will bestow upon the suppli-
‘cant who prays for it. Similarly, Baruch utters a petition for enlighten

$ Apparently the insight and understanding which are so highly

ment,
prized by the Apocalyptists are of such nature that they may be imparted
to none but the prayerful, * Baruch's prayer is offered in response to a
peculiarly confused vision which has appeared to him and which he can-

not interpret. In response to his prayer for divine explanation, the
Seer receives an answer: “And he,..said unto me: 'This is the interpre-
tation of the vision which thou hast seen."° So enlightenment and under
Standing, just as the more hypostasized Wisdom, in the Wisdom of Solomon,
are forthcoming as a result of petition. In his prayer, Baruch attributes
to God the ability to enlighten the human mind: "O Lord, my Lord," says

the Seer, "Thou dost always enlighten those who are led by understanding,

Thy law is life, and Thy wisdom is right guidance." ® The Seer thus doubly

attests to his belief in petitions for understanding, i,e., he prays
specifically for enlightenment and asserts within the supplication itself
that God affords understanding to those who seek and deserve it. Ben
Sirach likewise describes graphically his search for Wisdom through
prayer;
When I was very young, before I went on my wanderings,
I sought Wisdom expressly in my prayer;
In front of the temple I asked for her,
And I will search for her unto the end,

Other petitions for enlightenment appear in IV Ezra, ‘The Seer
grapples, in the opening section of the book, with a fundamental but
confused problem; "Why should God on the one hand spare the sinful, un
godly nations, and on the other hand ruthlessly punish his own people,
the more righteous Israel?" This introductory chapter thus places the
essential problem of the book in the form of an interrogation. Although
the chapter which poses the question is nowhere, in the traditional trans—
lation, called a "prayer," we shall classify it as such. In several ways
this lengthy section does not adhere to the accepted forms of supplication,
and perhaps it is with real justification that Oesterley has entitled it
a "perplexity." Yet because the words are obviously directed toward God
and assume the form neither of a dialogue nor of @ soliloquy, they consti-
tute a prayer. The chapter does not describe a conversation engaged
in by both God and the Seer, nor a meditation in which Ezra talks only
to himself, Since the Seer frequently employs the words "Thou" or "Thine,"

suggesting that the utterance is directed toward divinity, we may

call these verses a “prayer.” Moreover, the words "wisdom" or "enlighten-

ment" are not included, but because Ezra directs pointed questions to

Goa! ana obviously desires an answer, this is a prayer for understanding
and illumination, Although he is not asking for the interpretation of

a vision, as was Baruch, Ezra seeks instead to know why God allows

the wicked to prosper. Thus the basic problem of the book is outlined
in this long petition for enlightenment,

Likewise, in IV dzra 4:51f., another prayer is cast in the form
of a question, The Seer asks how long he will live and whether he will
be still alive on the day of judgment. Although the angel Uriel, who
has been sent to interpret the visions, does not give a definite answer,
-the petition which Salathiel offers actuslly implies a request for wisdom
and enlightenment,

But even more numerous than petitions for intellectual illumination
were those for victory and vengeance, chiefly military. Such prayers
were no exclusive and unique product of the intertestamental period,
for even in the early Old Testament times military chieftains, when

desiring victory, frequently offered supplications requesting it. John

Calvin briefly discusses prayers of this type. In his Institutes of the
Christian Religion, he concludes his elaborate discussion of the rules
for effective prayer with the following paragraph:

Te Scripture relates that the Lord has complied with some
prayers, which nevertheless did not arise from a calm or well-
regulated heart. Jotham, for a just cause indeed, but from the
impulse of rage, resentment, and revenge, devoted the inhabitants
of Shechem to the destruction which afterwards fell upon them
(Judges ix, 20)....Samson also was hurried away by a similar fervour
when he said 'O0 Lord, strengthen me, that I may be avenged of the
Philistines (Judges xvi. 28).' For though there was some mixture
of honest zeal, yet it was a violent, and therefore sinfyl, avidity
of revenge which predominated, God granted the request.

But such petitions are common in intertestamental times, The plot
of the Book of Judith centers around a particularly potent prayer for
vengeance, - In fact, Judith asks God to aid her in the deceit which
her lips utter and her actions betray so that she can overthrow the
oppressor, The Lord must dash down the strength and greatness of the
enemy, The spiteful prayer closes with the dramatic words:
Yea, yea, God of my father, and God of the inheritance of

Israel, Lord of the heavens and of the earth, Creator of the

waters, King of every creature, hear thou my prayer: and make

my speech and deceit to be their wound and stripe, who have pur-

posed hard things against thy covenant, and thy hallowed house...

And make every nation and tribe of thing to know that thou art

God, the God of all power and might....
This indeed is a powerfully worded petition, asking God to assert
Himself by striking down that enemy. Thus the prayer begins by recalling
how God hes taken vengeance in the past and concludes by requesting
Him to do so agein. In Judith's petition both victory and vengeance are
requested, but the emphasis is upon the latter. In First and Second
Maccabees this order is reversed, Undoubtedly there is desire for
revenge in the Maccabean prayers, but generally their greatest interest
is in victory. The fervent supplication uttered by the people at
Nicanor's attempt to enter the Temple, contained the element of vengeance,
First the priests and later Judas himself seek revenge, not for wrongs
done to them personally, but because Nicanor has blasphemed against the

Temple, In their prayer the priests, significantly enough, cry out

these words: "take vengeance on this man and his army, and let them fall

by the sword; remember their blasphemies, and suffer them not to live
any longer,"+ Later, when Judas prays, his emphasis is similar: "ven
so crush this army before us today...."” These are earnest supplications
for both victory and vengeance,

The author of II Maccabees portrays Judas as praying for victory
in many of his major battles. Immediately before (1) he attacks the forts
of Idumea, killing twenty-thousand of the enemy (10:16), (2) he fights the
hated Timotheus at Gazara (10:26), (3) he struggles against Lysias, the
King's guardian, at Bethsura (11:6), (4) he defeats the governor of Idumea,
Gorgias, at Mareshah (12:36f.), and (5) he finally overcomes Nicanor, the
last of his great antagonists, just outside Jerusalem (15:22-27), Judas
offers actual prayers for victory. Furthermore, the account, although
not specifically asserting that he prayed, suggests that Judas "called
upon the Lord" when he first organized his army (8:2), when he hoped the
Jews which had been taken captive by Nicanor might be freed (8:14-16),
when he attacked the murderers of Joppa (12:5), when he attacked the
fortified city of Caspin (12:15), when he overcame King Antiochus Bupator
(13:9-17), and when he learned that Nicanor was sending his first expedi-
tion against him (14:15). In only four cases does Judas fail to offer
some kind of prayer or at least to call upon the Lord: (1) when, early
in his career, he engages in his first minor raids (8:6,7); (2) when he
battles against Timotheus and Bacchides (8:30-33); (3) when he struggles
against the Arabs (12:10-12); and (4) when he marches against the cities
of Carnaim and Bphron-(12:26f). In II Maccabees, therefore, prayers
for victory are extremely numerous, Thus by 50 B. C., the date of compo~

sition for II Maccabees, petitions for victory were customary, Whether

the prayers are historical and actually uttered by Judas, or are
merely written into the account by the author, their very presence
in II Maccabees indicates that they were commonly accepted as legiti-
mate aids for producing military victories,

Finally, there were petitions for protection against evil spirits,
Although in many ways the prayer which Noah utters requesting the des-
truction of the demons is intercessory in nature, it is essentially
a petition, The evil sons of the fallen Watchers are leading Noah's
children astray; so for his sake and theirs he pleads:

Thou knowest how Thy Watchers, the fathers of these spirits,
acted in my day: and as for these spirits which are living, im-
prison them and hold them fast in the place of condemnation, and
let them not bring destruction upon the sons of thy servant, my Goa, +

Compared with Tobit, where magical devices are thought sufficient
to drive away evil spirits, Noah's prayer is highly advanced. Prayer
to God, not magical incantations, can control the spirits. In fact,
within the petition itself Nosh utters some illuminating words: "And
let them not rule over the spirits of the living; for Thou alone canst
exercise dominion over them, "* Unfortunately, however, the author of
this Noah-fragment is not entirely consistent, for subsequently in the
same chapter he implies that magical devices are efficacious against the
spirits.°© Noah is shown all of the necessary medicines and herbs and
all the acu of healing his children: “Thus the evil spirits were pre-

cluded from (hurting) the sons of Noah."* Magic and prayer run hand in

hand here, for, in response to the supplication, nine-tenths of the
Spirits are carried away and bound, but the other tenth remain end are
rendered harmless by the herbs, Abraham, also, just as he is preparing
to leave Haran, prays, and one of the elements of his petition is a
request for deliverance from the hands of evil spirits.1 Petitions were
frequent, therefore, in many of the writings of our period. Thomas Walker
has listed the many petitions which appear in Ben Sirach alone: "Prayer
might be for...self-control, or enlightenment on the difficult duties

of a counsellor, or justice for the poor, or recovery of the sick

(Cf, Sir, xxiii, 3-6; xxvii, 15; xxxviii, 9-14)."" Other books
contain a similar number of petitions. We have discovered prayers

for short-life, increased population, mercy, Wisdom, and victory. Thus
the predominant type of prayer is the request the expression of a desire,
the petition.

Next in frequency, variety, and importance are the intercessory
prayers. Again, this form of supplication is not exclusively the product
of the intertestamental period, for even in Old Testament days inter~
cession was not entirely unknown, According to John Calvin, the Jews
felt there must always be a Mediator between them and God.

For this reason the Lord had appointed in the Law that the
priest alone should enter the sanctuary, bearing on his shoulders
the names of the tribes of Israel, and the same number of precious
stones before his breast; but that the people should stand without
in the court, and there unite their prayers with those of the priest.

Tous the sacred ceremony suggests that the Jews felt themselves in need

of a Mediator who would carry on his shoulders, and bind to his breast,

symbols representing the people, Calvin here refers to Exodus 28, and though
his suggestion is illuminating, it is inisleading to affirm that the Jews
always sensed a need for a Mediator, Surely few of the patriarchs and the
prophets believed it either necessary or desirable to have a Mediator oper-
ating between themselves and God, Some intercessory prayers there assuredly
were, but most petitions were directed openly and immediately to God,
Calvin is more nearly correct when, in his discussion of the saints as
mediators, he urges that the prayers of the Jews were never pointed
directly toward the saints. They never prayed to their dead heroes and
patriarchs, Rather than requesting the intercession of the departed saints,
the Israelites "put the Lord in remembrance of his covcenant, in which
their most merciful Father has engaged to be propitious and beneficent to
them, for the sake of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, #14 Israel, according to such
an interpretation, did not pray directly to the departed spirits of her fathers;
instead she besought God to remember His servants, Abrahem, Isaac, and Jacob
and thus to respond to the prayers of their posterity. In the two sections
quoted, Calvin asserts that, although the people of Israel never expected the
spirits of their deceased heroes to intercede on their behalf, they believed
that the function of the priest was to intercede, praying to God for them,
Whether or not Calvin's statements concerning the Old Testement
are accurate, intercessory prayers occur frequently in the intertesta-
mental period, When studying such supplications, it is essential to
distinguish two fundamental types: (1) prayer by the living in behalf
of either their earthly comrades or their departed forebears, (2) prayer
by spiritual beings--either the “souls of the righteous" or the angels-~

for earthly men. Each of these classes is found prominently throughout

the literature of the period, but the later writings have more

examples of both kinds than do the earlier, The first instance of
intercessory prayer uttered by living men occurs in Jubilees. Moses
prays for Israel immediately after God has talked with him? Actually
the prayer is a petition in which Moses requests God not to forsake
Israel; yet it is also an intercession because the great patriarch asks
nothing for himself, praying only in behalf of his nation, Likewise,
after the evil spirits have led Noah's sons astray, he prays for their
protection and deliverance; "Let Thy grace be lift up upon my sons, and
‘let not wicked spirits rule over them lest they should destroy them from
the earth,"# Obviously the prayer (Jubilees 10:3) is essentially
intercessory; yet Noah also prays for himself, employing the words;
"But do thou bless me and my sons, that we may increase and multiply and
replenish the earth." One finds here, therefore, 4 natural admixture
of personal petition and pure intercession, But whether it be Moses
praying for Israel or Nosh praying for his sons, the implications are
the same; i.e., the man with "spiritual standing" before God intercedes
for the common, less-religious folk, Noah, in fact, expresses the con-
viction that God Sout od him particular portection at the time of the
flood, He not only believes himself to be specially “chosen” but thinks
that the efficacy of his intercession is most likely to be enhanced
because of this unique status, Moses was likewise an elect one of God,
and his intercession for Israel was undoubtedly influenced by his own
recognition of that fact. ‘The man with "standing" in the eyes of God

prayed for those who were more nearly strangers to Him, The Testaments
pasate RAE eS Le

of the Twelve Patriarchs, a book somewhat similar to Jetitess both

in style and purpose, refer to intercessory prayers resembling those

of Noah and Moses, They are the prayers of a righteous man in behalf

of sinners, Jacob prays for Reuben who has been stricken with a plague

because of his wickedness (Testament of Reuben 1:7), for Judah who has

transgressed grievously through his love for money (Judah 19:2), and for

Gad who because of his sin has become afflicted with a liver disease

(Gad 5:9). Jacob saves Gad's life as a result of this last intercession.

In the Testament of Benjamin (3:6) Jacob intercedes for Joseph's sons,

at the request of Jospeh himself. Jacob was heralded as a righteous man;

hence from the frequency with which he is said to have interceded for

Sinners, we may deduce that the intertestamental Jews believed the prayers

of the righteous in behalf of the unrighteous to be most efficacious,
The Apocalypse of Baruch similarly suggests that one's sins may

be forgiven as the result of the prayers of others;

Nay more, we were in our own land, and they helped us when we
sinned, and they prayed for us to Him who made us, because they
trusted in their wozky and the Mighty One heard sete prayer and
was gracious unto us,

The "they" refers to the "righteous men" and the “holy prophets" of pre-
Exilic days; hence it was men who were righteous, who trusted in their
"works," that prayed for the sins of their kinsmen. In the Book of Baruch,
it is the Hp not a patriarch, who utters a prayer for the people's

transgressions. In this book directed to the high priest at Jerusalem

by Baruch and the Exiles, the following exhortation appears: "Pray for

us to the Lord our God, for we have sinned against the Lord our God,
and to this day the anger of the Lord and his wrath have not turned
away from us," There are several possible interpretations to such a
prayer. Perhaps the people felt that because of their sins the petitions
they had uttered in their own behalf had been ineffective, God's anger
still being unappeased, On the other hand, they may have believed that
prayers offered in Jerusalem were more potent than those uttered else-
where, Finally, the Exiles may have thought that the orayers of a high
priest were more valid than were those of any other person, But what-
ever the interpretation of the verse, this much is certain: the high
priest was asked to intercede in behalf of sinful Exiles who felt that
their own supplications were not sufficient.

The Wisdom of Solomon relates the tale of a "blameless" man who

Rvetdenes for the righteous ones of Israel. Both the sinners and the
"just" are perishing from a plague, but the greater concern is expressed
over the welfare of the righteous, The reference in Wisdom is a Midrashic
interpretation of Aaron's activities in Numbers 16:47, The intertestament-

al writer presents the details as follows:

But it befell the righteous also to experience death,

And a multitude were stricken in the wilderness:

Howbeit the wrath endured not for long.

For a blameless man hasted to be their champion:

Bringing the weapon of his ministry,

Even prayer and the propitiation of incense.

He withstood the indignation and_put an end to the calamity,
Showing that he was thy servant.

Although the prayer was not unaccompanied by ritualistic acts, the fact

of Aaron's "blamelessness" is considered, along with intercession, the
factor most largely responsible for the miraculous cure of the Isrzel-
ites. God has been angry with Israel, the righteous as well as the
Sinner, but the "blameless" Aaron withstands His indignation, putting
an end to the calamity and appeasing His wrath, ‘The intercessory
prayers of the righteous for both the sinner and the sinless were
indeed thought to be powerful!

Furthermore, just as Aaron's supplication brings God's wrath to
an end, so Baruch's prayers are as "g strong wall" to Jeruselem, pro.
‘tecting her from His anger. God tells Baruch: "Your works are to this
city as a firm pillar, and your prayers as a strong wal1."+ Divine
truths are revealed to him for two reasons: (1) so that he may tell them
to Jeremiah; (2) so thet he (Baruch) may retire from Jerusalem, God
specifically desires the Seer to leave the city because his "works"
and his prayers are shielding her, As might be expected in this book,
which places so much emphasis upon merit and demerit, “works” are effi.
cacious in protecting the city. But prayer seems equally essential.
Baruch's supplications are called "a strong wall," and the reference to
them is placed at a climactic position within the verse: i.e., at the
end.* The function of a wall in ancient cities was to protect the en-
closed territory from the enemy, The implication of this verse, then,
is that the city cannot be destroyed so long as Baruch is praying there.
Although the word "intercession" is nowhere employed in this short chapter,

the context suggests that Baruch has been interceding in behalf of the

city. He, a just and honoreble man, has requested that God preserve
Jerusalem from destruction; so long as the Seer thus prays, the Lord
cennot exercise His wrath against the Jews, But now He is revealing
| the future to Baruch in order that the latter may retire from the
city, leaving it vulnerable to divine retribution,

An interesting variation occurs in II Maccabees, At the Battle
of Adullam many of Judas' men have fallen, and he discovers that the
disaster has come upon them because they have worn the forbidden amu-
lets of the idols of Jamnia, In their perplexity, the army that re-
mains intercedes in behalf of those who have fallen, “begging that the
Sin that had been committed should be wholly blotted out." The
living, by thus praying for their dead companions, evidence belief that
prayer can become part of the process of atoning for the sins of the
dead, The first prayers for the deceased in Israel's thought are con-
nected with the idea of eer tise. The writer of II Maccabees says;

For if he had not expected that those who had fallen would
rise again, it would have been superfluous and foolish to pray for
the dead; or if it was through regard for the splendid reward
destined for those who fall asleep in godliness, it was a holy and
pious thought, Therefore he made atongnent for the dead, so that
they might be set free from their sin.

Because only one clear-cut reference to prayer for the dead appears in
this literature, one might be led to conclude that such intercessions
were relatively unknown, Yet the author does not tell the incident as
though he thought it to be unique, Quite the reverse! He uses the
fact that Judas prayed for the dead as a proof for resurrection of the

pody.® If Judas’ prayer were in itself umsual, it is not likely that

it would have been pointed out as a proof-text. An opposite inter-
pretation may be conceivable, however, Prayers for the dead along with
belief in resurrection may have been so uncommon that the writer of
II Maccabees, seeking proof for his Pharisaic belief in resurrection,
and finding little evidence of prayer for the dead, points to the fact
that Judas allegedly prayed for his departed soldiers, If prayers for
the deceased were common, why should the author have attempted to link
this particular story with teachings about the resurrection? Was it
not to find the name of some hero who had prayed for the dead and had
- believed in resurrection? But whether intercession for the dead was so
common that the author tells the story of Judas! prayer with no explanation
about its uniqueness, or whether it was so rare that he chooses the story of
Judas to prove his great belief in immortality, much later dogma has been
built upon this tale in II Maccabees. Thus, not only were there prayers
of the living for the living, but in at least one recorded instance,
the living interceded for the dead. Furthermore, most of these suppli-
Cations concerned forgiveness of sins, Such is to be expected, however,
for Israel believed that calamaties ead as a direct result of trans.
eressions, If those disasters were to be removed, the sins must be for.
given, Therein lies the reason for the preponderance of intercessory
prayers uttered by the righteous in behalf of the unrighteous.

Perhaps the patriarch whose name is most frequently associated
with such intercession is Moses, who, according to Joshua, prayed in

Israel's behalf every day. Joshua, referring to a later generation, says

boldly:

They have no advocate to offer prayers on their hehalf to the
Lord, as did Moses the great messenger, who every hour day and night
had his knees fixed to the earth, praying and looking for help to
Him that ruleth all the world with compassion and righteousness,
Calling to mind the covenant of the fathers and propitiating the Lord
with the oath,
So well-known were his prayers of intercession while he lived on earth
that in later times he was thought of as fulfilling a similar function
in the spiritual world. "During his life he was Israel's intercessor
with God....But his relation to Israel did not cease with his death; he
Was appointed by God to be their intercessor in the spiritual world,..."©
The Book of Jubilees records a prayer uttered by Moses before his death
in behalf of Israel. He pleads for God not to forget His inheritance
and urges Him to have mercy upon Israel.° Thus the activity of Moses
serves as a transition between the two types of intercession which we have
thought to exist: (1) prayers of the living; (2) prayers of the deceased
"righteous ones" and the angels. Moses not only interceded while he lived
on the earth, but he was one of the great spiritual intercessors after he
died, To the deceased Jeremiah, likewise, this office of praying on
pehalf of Israel is frequently ascribed,* It is in II Maccabees that
the former High Priest Onias had just made intercession for all of
Israel:

Ten in the same fashion another man appeared, distinguished
by his gray hair and dignity, and wrapped in marvelous, most majestic

sublimity; and Onias answered and said,
‘Mis is Jeremiah, the prophet of God, who loves the brothers

and preys fervently for the people and the holy city.
In regard to the assignment of the intercessory function to the living
and deceased patriarchs, R, H. Charles comments:

This was a genuinely Jewish conception, and not borrowed

from Christianity, Thus,..,Jeremiah was held to discharge this

office in the spiritual world, 2 Macc. xv, 14. noch also (Slav.

Hn, Mss. AB lxiv. 5) was conceived of as 'one who removes the

sins of men,’ Philo speaks of the intercessions and prayers of-

fered on behalf of Israel by the righteous forefathers of the nation:

these intercessions of the departed saints of Israel were to be

the second of the three chief means for the restoration of their

descendants....
Walker insists that, rather than Moses, it was chiefly Enoch and Jere-
wiah who were looked to as spiritual intercessors;° yet he too lists
Moses as the "people's advocate to offer prayers on their behalf to the
Lord," But, whatever the name of the patriarch or father who uttered
the prayer, these glorified men were frequently substituted for the inter-
cessory angels,

It is in the Book of Enoch that intercessory prayers by angels are
most prominent. Both in 1-36 and 37-72 (the Similitudes) Enoch describes
the intercession of angels for men. Although in many essentials these
two sections of Enoch are dissimilar--forcing the conclusion that they
come from different writers and periods,--they both mention angelic in-
tercessors, According to Mnoch 1-36, it is the four great Archangels
and the Watchers who intercede for men, while in the Similitudes both
the Archangel Gabriel and the regular angels are intercessors, In the
former section, Michael, Uriel, Raphael, and Gabriel, looking down from

paradise and discovering the bloodshed and lawlessness upon the earth,

diy Maccabees 15:13f.

“Charles, The Assumption of Moses, op. cit., Pp. 49,

SWalker, op, cit., p. 12.

pray to God, 2 All four of them are represented as having uttered the
petition, and probably no one of them was set apart as the supreme
Intercessor, The prayer is from some view points embellished and re-
dundant, but its purpose is clear: i.e., to bring to God's attention

the horrible conditions that have arisen among men because of the sins
of the Watchers and to ask Him what ought to be done, The Archangels do
not ask God to forgive men's sins nor to protect them from the enemy

but to hear the cry of the righteous for justice, The angels thus

act as mediators, bringing before God the anguished cries of men for
deliverance from the throes of lawlessness, noch 15:2 implies that,
although the Watchers have rendered themselves unworthy by their sin-
fulness, they ought ideally to take their place along side the Archangels
as intercessors, When speaking about these sinful Watchers, God says to
Enoch, “And go, say to the watchers of heaven, who have sent thee to
intercede for them: you should intercede for men, and not men for you, "©
The suggestion is, of course, that the Watchers, except for their sin,
could have been intercessors, and that they may once have fulfilled their
rightful function in those mythical days before the Fall. Furthermore,
it implies that even among the heavenly hosts only the righteous can |
operate effectively as intercessors, Sinless Watchers could intercede,
but the fallen could not.

The Similitudes closely resemble Enoch 1-36 in many of these
emphases, When Enoch is carried away by the wind to the end of the
heavens, he sees the righteous and holy ones praying and interceding for
men, ‘The author is not explicit as one might wish and he does not
state whether these righteous ones are merely the souls of the departed

Saints of Israel or whether they are angels, It may be, indeed, that
the word "angel" is merely another name for the souls of the righteous
dead. Yet such a contention is difficult to maintain in the light of the
author's description of the scene:
Here mine eyes saw their dwellings with His righteous angels,

and their resting-places with the holy, and they petitioned, and

interceded and prayed for the children of men, and righteousness

flowed before them as water, and mercy like dew upon the earths

thus it is they fare for ever and ever,1
Because these righteous ones are dwelling with God's engels there must be
a distinction of some degree between them and the angels: so this is not
a clear-cut reference to intercessory prayers by the latter. Instead,
Enoch attempts to describe the glorious future awaiting the just men of
earth, But whether or not these righteous ones whom he sees may legit-
imately be called angels, they are heavenly beings, abiding with the
angels, and exercising the privilege of interceding for men,

A more certain allusion to angelic intercessors occurs in Enoch 4036,

The Seer, having been given a view of the heavenly glory, says "and the
third voice I heard pray and intercede for those who dwell on the earth
and supplicate in the name of the Lord of Spirits."“ ‘This archangel's
function is to make intercession on behalf of the eartn's inhabitants,
Yet only in Enoch 40:9 is this third voice given the name of "Gabriel."
Whereas in the section 1-36 the four Archangels pray, the Similitudes
assign to Gabriel alone the task and the honor, In Bnoch 47:2, however,

the confusion between the righteous ones and the angels reappears, Charles

in this instance calls the prayers "the angels! intercession."° But the

same terminology is employed as appears in 59353

And in those days shall have ascended the prayer of the righteous,

And the blood of the righteous from the earth before the Lord of Spirits,

In those days the holy ones who dwell above in the heavens

Shall unite with one voice

And supplicate and pray (and praise,

And give thanks and bless the name of the Lord of Spirits)

On behalf of the blood of the righteous which has been shed,

And that the prayer of the righteous may not be in vain before the

Lord of Spirits,

That judgnent may be done unto then,

And that they may not have to suffer for ever.+
The righteous of earth first pray to the Lord of Spirits; then the holy
ones who dwell in the heavens intercede so that the petitions may be
heard. It cannot be definitely determined whether the intercessors are
‘true angels or ere just the souls of the deceased righteous men. But
the author makes no distinctions between the two classes of heavenly
beings. He says: “the holy ones who dwell above in the heavens shall
unite with one voice,"© Surely the angels may be listed as "holy ones
that dwell above in the heavens" just as can the souls of the dead. The
author of Enoch implies that all of the hosts of heaven pray; so whether
they are called "angels" or not is hardly important. The angels and
holy ones both intercede for men,

Not only Enoch 1-36 and 37-72 refer to the intercession of the
angels, but the fifth section (91-104) twice points to a somewhat similar
kind of supplication. At the heart of a series of woes pronounced on the
ungodly, Enoch suggests that the righteous must pour out their prayers
before the angels "that they may place the sin of the sinners for a
memorial before the Most High, "> Although this reference is not entirely

lucid, its meaning is clarified by a similar verse: "I swear unto you,

that in heaven the angels remember you for good before the glory of the

Great One."! In neither of these sentences does the word "intercession"
appear, nor, indeed, is there any outright suggestion that the angels
offer a prayer of any kind, They are to hear the supplications of the
righteous and are then to bring the sins of the godless before the Most
High, so that He may punish the wicked and hear the prayer of the godly.”
Such action on the part of the angels is not actually intercessory
prayer but is a form of mediation, The angels operate as "go-betweens,"
helping God to hear the prayers of deserving men and to punish sinners.
It is preateea in 10431 that the angels will, without fail, remember the
righteous before the Lord, But nowhere is it suggested that they will
offer intercessory prayer. God will be propitiated by direct mediation
rather than by supplication. The angels will appear before Him and, in
addition to relaying petitions to Him, will represent the righteous
Israelites in His presence.

Departing even more radically from the idea of strict intercessory
prayer is the following section from the Testament of Levi:

In the heaven next to it are tne archangels who minister and
make propitiation to the Lord for all the sins of ignorance of the
righteous; offering to the Lord a syeet-smel ling savour, 2
reasonable and a bloodless offering.

A sacrificial service in heaven is implied, Prayer may of course be
uttered as an element in such an observance, but the emphasis rests

upon the act of sacrifice. Again it is the Archangels who administer

the propitiation, and it is the sins of the righteous, rather than of the

ungodly, that are concerned, But it is to be an "offering" not a sup-
plication, Elsewhere in the Testaments of the Twelve Patriarchs there
are references to interceding angels who propitiate notso much by
prayer as by sacrificial acts or by representing the people directly
while standing in the presence of God, In response to Levi's question,
his angelic guide informs him: "I am the angel who intercedeth for the
nation of Israel that they may not be smitten utterly, for every evil
spirit attacketh it,"1 ‘The word "prayer" again does not appear, nor
are we led particularly to believe that the task of this angel is to
supplicate for Israel, He acts as protector by directly appearing
before God and appeasing His wrath. Furthermore, Dan, speaking to his
children, says: "Draw near unto God and unto the angel that inter-
cedeth for you, for he is a mediator between God and man..." When
we contrast such a verse to many of those in Enoch which record actual
intercessory supplications and consider prayer and intercession to be
partners,° one may conclude that not prayer, but some more direct form
of mediation, is implied by these references in the Testaments of the
Twelve Patriarchs. Hence, although mediation of the angelic beings usual-~
ly suggests intercessory prayer, such is not invariably the case. Now
and again some more direct form of "standing in the presence" of God
is implied. Interposed between'God's wrath and the destruction of

Israel, these angelic mediators are not so much supplicants as they are

the eyes and ears of God.

The Book of Tobit suggests that Raphael, one of the seven angels,
brings the remembrance of Sareh's and Tobit's prayers to God. "So
now, when you and your daughter-in-law Sarah prayed, I brought the
remembrance of your prayer before the Holy One." Again, Raphael,
when offering his great apologia, says: "For I am Raphael, one of the
seven holy angels, who offer up the prayers of God's people and go into
the presence of the glory of the Holy One," No more lucid statement
could be desired. Raphael is an intercessor, not because he prays in
behalf of Israel, but because he hears Israel's supplication and brings
them before God, thus becoming Israel's representative in the Court of
the Lord,

Although intercessory prayers are not exclusively a product of
the intertestamental period, having probably been known in the litera
ture of the Old Testament, they are an important type of supplication,
If the Book of Tobit and Enoch 1-36 sre two of the earliest, and the
Testament of the Twelve Patriarchs one of the latest, of our writings,
intercession was a feature of Jewish prayer life which persisted throughout
the intertestamental period, Righteous Jews may intercede on behalf of
others, while angels may pray for living men, But angelic intercession,
as we have seen, does not always imply prayer. The righteous upon earth
having prayed to God, the angels may either pray to Him on their behalf
or mediate by representing the righteous men's cause directly before God,

Yet the angels bring human desires and needs before the Lord only after

men have prayed,

Of lesser interest, but nevertheless important, are the several
prayers of repentance which occur in this literature, Nearly two
hundred and fifty years intervene between I Esdras and I Baruch; yet
in each of these writings there are prayers of penitence and humiliation.
Appearing as the sole long supplication in I Esdras, the first of these
prayers, uttered by Ezra when he is informed that the men of Israel
have not put away their foreign wives as commanded, is extremely inter-
esting. When Ezra ae that the Jews still live with their Gentile
partners, he rends his clothes, plucks his beard, and prays, It is one
man praying for the sins of a nation. To that extent, such a supplication
may well be called an intercession. Yet it is far more than that, It
opens with the words: “Lord, I am ashamed, I am abashed before you.

For from the times of our forefathers, our sins have risen higher than

our heads and our mistakes have mounted up to heaven, and we have been
involved in grievous sin, even to this day." Ezra prays not only

for Israel in general, but also for himself, continually employing the
words "our" and "we." The closing phrases of the prayer likewise ex-
press a desire for forgiveness and an inward attitude of repentance:

"Now here we are before you, in all our iniquity, for in view of it we
cannot any longer stand before you, "” Recognizing his own and his people's
sins, Ezra comes to God with a desire to repent and centers his suppli-
cation around that desire. So in a sense his was a prayer of both con-

fession and repentance, which are manifestly related,

Vastly different is the single prayer of penitence which occurs
in Enoch, The Mighty Ones and the Kings will, on the last day, repent
of their sins and confess them to God,

And they will bless and glorify the Lord of Spirits,

and say: ‘Blessed is the Lord of Spirits, the Lord of Kings,

the Lord of the mighty and the Lord of the rulers, the Lord of

glory and the Lord of wisdom, (before whom) every secret is clear,

And Thy power is from generation to generation end Thy glory for

ever and ever: deep are all Thy secrets and innumerable, and

Thy righteousness is beyond reckoning, We have now learnt that

we should eee | and bless the king of kings and Him who is king

over all kings,’
Tous the wicked, on the day of judgnent, will confess their sin by
praying to God, It will be seen, however, that the element of praise
is more prominent than any expression of repentance. The context into
which the prayer is set implies that these mighty ones and kings will
desire to confess their shortcomings, but the prayer itself has little
that suggests a penitent attitude except the fi
